That was the whole of it. It was a place, and nothing was owed for it, and there was no jacket on a peg.

"Top rung," said Birch now. "Yarrow has the cloth. He kneels where you put him, and you ask him. I'm told that's how it's done by people who mean to bag anything."

"Yes, sir."

He walked out onto the blue brick, dark.

He disliked it at once. It was not the string. He had cut that string's top rung nine times since the seventh month, and he knew how it stood. It was the floor. There was something coming up through his boot soles, slow and even, like the tread of a great many people going past on the other side of a wall. His feet wanted to fall in with it.

He had the best feet in the School. He had been told so since he was fourteen. It was a new thing to find that they could be asked to do something by a floor.

*Count against it,* he thought. He had watched a girl do that on the cinders of the Pit, with a hold's corner planted under her heel. He had not understood what she was at until a blade with a broken leg had explained it to a cart.

He counted in threes. It was the first number that came. His feet steadied.

Yarrow was laying the cloth. He was a still-door, long and incurious, and he threw a sheet as a man lays a table that he has laid before. He put it on the east side of the trough, hard up against the planks, under the string's top knot.

"That's close to the water," said Kellan.

"It's where it'll fall."

"It's a pace from a thing that's got two cords down in it."

"I've sixty counts," said Yarrow, patiently. "Ash doesn't run. It lies. It lies where it lands and leans to the nearest lamp, and I've put myself between it and the bench. What else is there?"

It was a fair answer. It was what the book said. Kellan looked at the trough, and at the white cords going down into it, and could not find the place in the book where it said anything else.

He looked along the east wall instead. He did it without quite meaning to. There was a catcher there who was particular about ground.

She had her eyes on the cloth. She was frowning. But she was not his catcher today, and she had not been asked, and he saw her shut her mouth on whatever it was.

"Renn," said Birch. "While we're young."

He went up the back of it dark, on his struts, in two bounds.

"Now?"

"Not yet," said Yarrow. "It's swaying. Now."

He lit.

It was four-tenths. He had it to a hair. The edge went through the top knot level and came out the other side, and he was dark again before his feet had found the brick. He had never made a cleaner one in that yard.

Sixty drams fell like sand out of a split sack.

"*Dark! One!*"

It did not lie.

He saw it from three paces. The heap hit the canvas and spread, as a heap does, and then the near edge of it began to move. It did not drift. It ran. It went across the waxed cloth toward the trough in a grey tongue, as flour runs to the low side of a tilted board, and over the hem, and up the planks.

It was climbing the cord.

"*Corners!*" Yarrow had the hem in both fists. His voice had gone up. "Corners, *now,* it's going—"

Pettit and Hesketh hauled. The neck came in. It closed on what was left, and Yarrow's cord went round it three times as fast as Kellan had ever seen a cord go.

"Sealed," said Yarrow. "Twenty—" He stopped. He weighed the bag in his two hands, as a catcher will. "That's not thirty. That's not anything like thirty."

There was a grey scum on the water.

It lay all down the trough, from the stool to the pump, moving with the rise and fall. Half a rung of open ash had gone into a foot of water inside ten counts.

"The bench," said Kellan's mother.

She said it quite quietly. He turned his head.

All six needles had jumped. He saw them do it, together, like six hands flung up. They stood for a moment against their upper pins, quivering. Then they lay down. They did not come down one after another, as cells die in a bank. They went as one, and the glass over the third cell cracked across with a small flat sound.

The pump stopped.

"That's fouled," said Rusk. His voice was thick. "That's one pinch of open ash in the water, and six cells cooked inside a breath. All six. There wasn't one of them that could say no to the others."

"There were six," said Mara. "I've four-and-twenty."

Nobody answered her.

Kellan stood on the blue brick with his blade dark on its cuff. He had cut a rung as well as he knew how, on the word of a catcher who had done what the book said, and it had killed a bank.

*I asked,* he thought. *I asked him and he told me, and it was a good answer.*

He had learned to ask. It had cost him a season. It had not until this moment crossed his mind that asking the catcher was no use at all when the catcher did not know either.

---

"The pump's dead," said Kiva.

She had not been asked. She had said it aloud all the same, since it was the thing that was there.

"We can see that the pump's dead," said Birch.

"The water isn't."

They looked.

The brewer's pump stood with its plunger down. The six cells on the bench lay cold. Nothing in that yard was drawing on the trough, and all down its twelve paces the grey water rose a finger on the lead, and fell.

In. And out.

Mara walked the length of it. She walked slowly, with her eyes on the wet line. She stopped at the north end, where the string stood astride the stool on its four legs, with five knots left and two white cords going down between them.

The cords were moving. They drew up, a hand, and let down. Three grey knots above them leaned back a little as they did it, and eased, like three people hauling a neck shut on a called word.

"It's pumping," said Mara.

She turned round. Her face was perfectly composed, and Kiva had never seen it so white.

"There. That's my half sheet. *Stop the pumps and the main lies still.* It doesn't. If there's one of those standing in it, the pumps may stop and the bank may cook, and the water goes on keeping time, since the thing's learned the stroke and has two arms down the hole." She picked the paper off the bench. "And I'd have filled four-and-twenty cells fresh, in the dark, on the strength of that sentence."

"Fill the one," said Rusk. "The stool. Show me the rest of it. I'd sooner see the whole of what I've got wrong."

A Works fitter did it, at arm's length, with the string's legs on either side of him and a ward of Birch's between. He quenched the cell on the stool, and drew it, and slid a fresh can home.

"Open," he said. "One. Two."

At forty the needle rose and stood, on a time of its own. The fitter let out his breath.

At fifty-one it trembled.

At fifty-eight it went up, and came down, with the water.

"Inside the count," said Pike. He was looking at it as he looked at a neck. "Fresh ash is open for a slow sixty. It takes the first pattern that touches it. I've said that to every corner-hand I ever trained, about a lit blade." He shook his square head. "I never thought to say it about a puddle."

"So I can't quench it while it's in the water," said Rusk. "And I can't stop the water while it's in the water. And I can't cut it while it's in the water, or the rung runs down its own arm and cooks my bank." He counted them off on his thumbs. "Then it must come out of the water. How does one take a thing like that by the arm?"

"You don't put a ward on it," said Greta Stroud.

She said it from the rail, hoarsely, without being asked.

"I've said that on two floors now. I'll say it on every one I'm ever stood on. I put a wall against a cord like that in the spring, and I meant to keep hold. It went up me to the elbows."

"Iron, then," said Rusk. "Dead iron. Something with nothing in it to take."

They tried iron. It was a fair thing to try.

The fitter had a boat-hook. He stood well back, on the west side, and reached across the trough with the full length of it, and got the hook under the nearer cord where it left the water.

Frost ran up the shaft like a fuse.

"*Let go!*" said Rhea Sorn.

He had let go already. He was not a fool. The boat-hook hung in the air for a moment, white from end to end, with nobody holding it. Then the cord drew up, a hand, and the hook went with it and was laid down in the water as gently as a spoon.

The fitter stood looking at his glove. The palm of it had gone stiff, and cracked when he bent it.

"That's my left," he said. "I'd like it noted that I'm right-handed."

"It's noted," said Perrin, with feeling, from the wall.

Nobody laughed much.

Kiva had been watching the cord.

She knew that rise and fall. She had had it in a tray between her palms, in the Pit, for one slack count, light as a clothesline. She had not held it. That was the whole point of what she had done. She had put a pan under it at the instant that it stopped pulling, and had been somewhere else before it began again.

"It's got a slack," she said. "Four counts hauling and four easing. A ward for one tap, on the slack, and folded before the haul comes back. It doesn't take a ward that isn't there any longer."

"That's a catcher's notion," said Pike.

"It's what I did. I only did it once."

"I know what you did. I was behind three posts with three cloths." He was not looking at her. He was looking along the east wall, at a small round first-year with a yellow card. "It wants the best fold in the yard. That isn't yours."

"No," said Kiva. "It's hers."

Pru Finch looked behind her, to see who was meant.

"Me?"

"You fold from the far edge. You don't let go. You've always got the last of it in your palms." Kiva heard that she was talking quickly, and slowed. "I learned it from you on a pail. I do it with two fingers stuck out like a fork. You'd do it properly."

"I can't go anywhere with it. I haven't an Ember. I can't put it down."

"Nobody's asking you to go anywhere. I'd like to know whether it can be lifted off the water at all. For a count. By a ward that isn't taken."

Pru looked at Rhea Sorn.

"Your card says a corner and a lamp," said Rhea. "And the cord. I underlined the cord on Friday." The pale eyes rested on her. "It doesn't say this. So it's yours to refuse, and I'll think none the worse. Stroud will tell you what it costs if it's late."

"Six weeks," said Stroud. "And a spoon held for you."

Pru swallowed. She put her card away in her tunic, and buttoned the pocket.

"I'd want it called," she said. "The slack. I can't watch the cord and my hands."

"I'll call it," said Kiva.

It was out before she had thought. Of course she would call it. It was a hem under load, with a count in it, and she was the catcher. She had called the count on every sheet that Seven had thrown since the fifth month.

She went and knelt at the rail of the trough, a long pace from the planks. She laid her hands on the blue brick, the right palm and the heel of the left.

It came up through the brick, slow and even. She could feel the haul in it. She could feel the easing.

Pru knelt on the east side, three paces from the string's near leg, with a ward of Birch's standing over her like a porch. She put up her round hands. The silver stood between them, no bigger than a tea tray, and she bent it into the bowl of a spoon.

"*Out,*" said Kiva. "Two. Three. Four. *In*—"

She knew as she said it.

It was late. It was not much late. It was the width of the difference between two hands, of which one told her the truth and the other told her that there was a floor. She had taken the middle of them, as she had for four years, and the middle was wrong by half a count.

Pru slid the pan under.

The cord was not slack. It was just beginning to draw. It came up into the bowl of that small bright ward with the whole haul of three knots behind it, and Kiva saw the silver go grey at the rim, like a window breathed on.

"*Fold!*" That was Pike, and Stroud, and Anwen, all at once.

Pru folded.

She did it faster than Kiva had ever seen anything done with a ward. Far edge. Near edge. A penny. Shut. The cord fell back into the water with a slap.

She sat down on the brick with her hands held up in front of her face.

They were white. They were white to the first joint, all eight fingertips and the two thumbs, as though she had dipped them in milk.

"They've gone to sleep," she said, in a high surprised voice. "They've all gone to sleep. I'm narrating. I can't feel my card. I had my card."

Ruddock was over the rail already, with a towel. Anwen was a pace behind him.

Kiva had not moved. She was still kneeling, with her hands on the brick.

"Who called that?" said Pike.

"I did."

"I know that you did. I want the rest of it."

She made herself say it to the whole yard, in a carrying voice, as she would have called a mark.

"I called the *in* half a count late. I had it through two hands and I took the middle. One of them can't hear." Her mouth was dry. "I told a yard on Friday night that I couldn't take a count any longer by myself. I said that somebody else would have to have it. Then I knelt down here and took it, since it's what I've always done. That's mine. It's her hands."

"It's the tips," said Ruddock, over his shoulder. "It's a nip. She folded inside it. Ten minutes, and she'll be able to pick her nose."

"I don't," said Pru faintly.

Kiva stood up. Her knees were not quite steady.

"Brask," she said. "You called the Pit's. Would you call this?"

Lowen Brask had been standing with Old Four, with his notebook shut. He came along the wall without hurry.

"I'd call it," he said. "I'd sooner you'd asked me five minutes ago. I was standing there with it in my mouth." He looked at her, quite kindly. "You said it on Friday in the Graywater yard, I'm told. It's all over the Hall. *The count of a thing. Somebody else has that.* It's a good rule. You'll have to keep it on the days that you're frightened as well."

"I know."

"I know you know. I'm only saying it as it was said to me." He turned to the trough. "Finch. When you're ready. Not before."

It was twelve minutes before the color came back. Pru would not be hurried, and nobody hurried her. Then she knelt down again in the same place, with her jaw set, and put up her hands.

"*Out,*" said Brask. His voice was as even as a clock. "Two. Three. Four. *In.*"

The pan went under.

For one count the white cord lay in a small silver spoon, a hand's breadth above the water, light as a clothesline.

"*Two.* Fold."

It was gone. The cord dropped.

And down the whole length of the trough, the water missed a beat.

Kiva saw it. Everybody saw it. The grey scum rose a finger on the lead, and fell, and rose, and then for the space of one slow breath it only lay there and rocked.

On the stool, between the string's knees, the single needle checked. It stood for a count. Then the water drew again, and it went on.

"There," said Kellan's mother, from her glass. "It fell out. For as long as that arm was off the water, that cell had nobody to follow. It came back in as soon as it was asked."

"Then it wants more than a tap," said Rusk slowly.

"It wants the arm kept off," said Mara. "Lifted, and taken away, and put down in dry ground, and held there. For as long as it takes a Works crew to do the rest." She did not look at the east wall. "I've seen that done. I sat in a gallery and watched it. I don't know of any other hand that can."

Nobody in the yard looked at Kiva.

It was a strange thing to be at the middle of. Forty people had all at once found something to study in the brick, or the lattice, or their own boots.

"I'll say it, since it's on my board," said Ysra Crest, from the gate. Her level voice carried the whole floor. "*It is not to be asked of her.* I wrote that under a result, with my name. I meant it. Nobody in this yard will ask."

"Nor will it be offered," said Rhea Sorn. "Not on my card."

She had turned her back on the trough. She was speaking to Vane, and to Rusk, and to a woman in a work apron, and not to a girl of seventeen.

"She's attached to me. That's my hand on her entry and my name against whatever becomes of her. I had her spend two fingers on a floor of mine three Saturdays ago, with the price read out first, and I told her afterward that I'd never once named a price before I cut." Her voice was flat. "I've named this one to myself. It's too dear. I'll not have a catcher of mine go up in the dark over a hole in the road and come down with half a hand, for a plan that had a wrong sentence in it an hour ago. Find me another way."

"I'd be glad to," said Mara. "What is it?"

"Plank the holes. Every one. Give it no water to put an arm in. Then it's a string on a street, and I take it down a rung at a time on a gate, as I've taken two hundred."

"And if it finds a hole that we didn't?"

"Then I'll cut it where it stands and bag what I can."

"With its arm down my drain. You watched what half a rung did to six cells."

"I watched."

They looked at one another. They were not angry. Kiva saw that, and it frightened her more than anger would have. They were two women who were each right about a thing, and the things did not fit.

She had an answer.

It was a good one. It was about a foreman with nobody south of a string, and a hand that was already spent, and what a catcher was for. It would have sounded very well on that brick.

She looked at Pru's fingers. They were pink again, and Pru was holding them up to the light one at a time, as though she had been given them for a present.

*I'd an answer ten minutes ago,* thought Kiva. *It was to kneel down and call it.*

She put the good one where she had put the others.

"Sorn's right that it isn't hers to have offered," she said. "I've nothing to say. I'd like to know how you get that one back in its cage."

Birch grunted.

"That's the first sensible question since two o'clock. I don't. Not with its arms in a trough." He jerked his head at the fitter. "There's a bung in the south end of that. Pull it."

The fitter pulled it.

The water went out across the blue brick in a grey sheet and found the yard's drain. The trough emptied in a slow minute. The wet line went down the lead, and down, and there was a foot of air where the water had been.

The two white cords hung in it.

They hung for perhaps ten counts, reaching, with nothing to reach. Then they simply let go. They came apart from the bottom, softly, into loose grey ash that pattered on the lead like rain.

The shine went back up onto the string's shoulders. Its blunt head lifted. It turned, a quarter of a circle, and found a wall of silver twelve paces to the west, and walked to it and leaned.

"Now it's a string," said Birch. "Now I know what to do with it."

"You took the water away," said Kiva.

"I pulled a bung. I can't pull a bung on the Ferren main."

"No." Mara had bent over the empty trough. She was looking at the lead, and the wet, and the little heap of grey sweepings where two arms had been. "No, nobody can drain a main. But one might shut a piece of it. A sluice. A gate, let down across the brick, with the hill on one side and the low streets on the other. They had them on every drain in this city before there were pumps." She straightened. "I've never seen one on that plan."

"The plan's been tidied," said Kiva.

"So it has."

"Why is it called Stopgate?"

She had not known that she was going to ask it. It had been lying in her head since Tuesday, with a great many other things that she did not know the use of.

Mara opened her mouth. She shut it again.

"I've no idea," she said. "I've walked up it for thirty years. I supposed that it was somebody's name."

---

Noa was on the Library step at eight on Monday, as he had said, and Kiva was beside him.

The Reader let them in herself. She was small and upright, with two pairs of spectacles, and she looked at Kiva as one looks at a borrower who has brought a book back clean.

"The post," she said. "I've been waiting for somebody from that School to ask me since 2649. I'd begun to think that I should have to put it in my will." She took a lamp from its hook. "Three flights. Mind your heads on the second."

The superseded stacks smelled of cold iron and paste. She went along a row without reading the labels and drew out a thick grey volume with a broken spine.

"The *Standard Lane Manual,* eleventh edition. Withdrawn 2603. There's the lane."

It was halfway down a page. It had the exercise set out in six ruled columns, in print, and a seventh squeezed in at the right.

*The lane with the crooked posts. Why: because the road isn't straight.*

Beside it, in pencil, in a very small hand, somebody had written a line. *Retired from standard ed., 2603. Committee vote 11–6. Original reason retained here.*

"That's a junior archivist," said the Reader. "She'd no leave to write in it. I've blessed her for it rather often."

"*Because the road isn't straight,*" said Noa. He read it twice, with his lips. "That isn't a reason. It's—"

"A riddle. So the committee said. It's in the minutes." The Reader turned back a leaf. "Now read me the other six columns."

Kiva read them. *What:* nine posts in two rows. *How:* run dark, on a count. *Went wrong:* a collision at the ninth. *Cost.* *Second try.* It was all there, tidy and complete.

"It says posts," she said slowly. "*The lane with the crooked posts.* There's only the one."

"Is there?" The Reader looked at her over the upper spectacles. "In the eleventh edition there's a plate. I'll show you."

It was an engraving, brown with age. It showed a strip of ground under a wall, with two rows of posts down it, and not one of them stood square to its neighbor. The whole lane curved. It bent like a river, from the near end to the far, and the last post of the south row stood a pace out from all the rest with something drawn across its top.

"That's a bar," said Noa. "Through the post. Like a capstan."

"The School straightened the lane in 2390, when it built the wall higher. It's in the bursar's accounts. *To setting the posts fair, eleven days.* They couldn't shift the last one. It doesn't say why." She shut the book. "And that's all that my shelf can give you. A plate, and a riddle, and a clerk's pencil. I can tell you that a reason was there. I can't tell you what it was. This is the manual. It kept the why and lost the what."

Somebody was coming down the iron stair.

He came at a walk. He had his hat in one hand and a long flat tin case under his arm.

"Then I've the other half," said Hollis Vane. "I was until two this morning finding it. It was in the chest under the bearer's box, with the Line's lane sheets, which nobody has unrolled since my grandfather was a boy." He laid the case on the reading shelf. "It's in the record hand. I can't read a word of it. I've brought it to the only person in Meridian who can."

The Reader put on the second spectacles over the first.

It was a single sheet, soft as cloth, ruled in seven columns. She bent over it for some time.

"This is older than my manual," she said. "It's a copy, in the record hand, of something a good deal older again. It's the same entry. It isn't the same words." She read it out, slowly, a column at a time.

"*The lane with the crooked posts. At Meridian, under the south wall, on the line of the hill drain.*

"*What: nine posts, set as the drain runs, which is not straight. The ninth is the sluice post. It stands out from the wall for the bar.*

"*How: run it dark, from the sheds. Call the turn before the eighth, aloud, and say who has the bar. Two to the bar. Nine turns shuts the hill from the low streets.*

"*Went wrong: ran it straight and silent, and met the ninth. Also: two came to the bar, and nobody had said which two.*

"*Cost: a collarbone. A cellar in the Hythe, the night that nobody had said.*

"*Why: because the road isn't straight. S.V.*"

Nobody spoke.

Kiva found that she had her right hand flat on the shelf, as she would have laid it on a pavement.

"It's a gate," she said. "The post. It isn't for practice. It's the handle of a gate." She could hear her own voice, and it sounded very young. "The drain turns there. I felt it turn. They put the lane on top of the drain so that everybody who ever trained in that yard would know the way to it in the dark. And they made you say it aloud. Which way, and who."

"*Call the turn before the eighth,*" said Vane.

He was looking at her.

"I stood in that yard on Saturday night and watched you make a carry team shout *post, left* over its shoulder. I thought that it was neat. I thought that you'd made it up." He put his hat down on the tin case. "You had. That's what I can't get past. Somebody wrote that down five hundred years ago, and a school lost it, and a committee voted on it, and you made it up again in a quarter of an hour because two litters hit a post."

"It was Perrin who said it was a lane call."

"I dare say it was." He turned to the Reader. "May that sheet go to the head of Stopgate? Today? I'll sign for it under the watch's name."

"It may go wherever the Line carries it. It's the Line's. I've only read it to you." She took off the second spectacles. "You'll want a spade, Officer. That plate shows the bar a hand above the ground. It's had a good many centuries of yard swept over its feet."

"I've a man with a saw," said Vane. "I told a sponsor so. I shall send him home and ask him whether he's a shovel."

---

Drum twenty-eight fell in at eighteen minutes to eight that evening, with thirty people at its rail to watch it.

Kiva was on the pavement. She had been told not to come nearer, and she did not need to. She saw it through the green door, past her aunt's shoulder.

The needle stood. It trembled. Then it went up, without any hurry, and came down. It had done it before anybody had drawn breath to say so.

Below it on the hill, behind two more green doors, two needles lay against their pins in a pair of drums that had been dry since Wednesday midnight.

The Chair's clerk had the paper. He broke the wax in front of Rusk and gave it to him.

Rusk read it aloud. He read it in a flat voice, to the wardens, and the fitters, and a pleasant man in a black coat who had taken off his hat.

"*If it is the drain: drum twenty-eight, on Monday the third of next month, with two dry drums between.*" He folded it along its crease. "It's the third. That's twenty-eight. Those are two dry drums." He looked down the street, at six hundred dim brown windows. "I've had six hundred families on boxes for five days, in the wet, to learn that I was wrong. I said that I'd carry that. I find that it's heavier than it looked on Wednesday."

"A sixth time," said Mr. Adlam. He said it very quietly. "I shall have to write to the technical office that there is a thing which its patent does not describe. They will not like it. I rather think that I do."

"You stopped nothing," said Mara, to Rusk. "And you did no harm. I'd have done the same in your chair, and I said so in that room."

"You did. I'd it written." He took off his linen cuff guards, one after the other, which she had never seen him do, and put them in his pocket. "It's two days to twenty-nine, and two more to a school. Mrs. Rowan. You called the drum. You found the drain. I'm told that you've a sheet of paper, as of this morning, with a sluice on it." He held out the trunk plan, rolled. "It's yours. The hill, the bank, and the stopping of it. I'll sign whatever you draw, and I'll put my forty operators where you say."

Mara did not take it at once.

"I don't want it," she said.

"No. I'd noticed. That's a good part of why."

She took it. She held it for a moment in her left hand, the reading one, as she would have held a can that might be cut.

Then she looked round for her clerk.

"Kiva," she said. "The book. And find me a spade."

---

## CHAPTER — editions/movement-006-pre-repair/chapter-44.md

# Chapter 44 — Kiva: Two to the Bar

The man with the saw turned out to be a perfectly good shovel, and by nine on Tuesday he had the cross dug up.

Kiva watched it go. She stood in the Old Yard with the book against her chest, and a stranger in a leather apron put a spade into the mark that she had come down on since the summer and turned it over like a garden bed.

"Two hands down," he said. "There's iron."

It was a lid. It was a yard square, of the old thick sort, with a ring let into it, and it lay at the foot of the leaning post on the side toward the lane. It had been under the packed dirt for so long that the dirt had taken the print of the ring.

Three fitters lifted it with bars. A smell came up, cold and green.

"Lamp," said Mara.

She lay down at the edge of it, in her work apron, and let the lamp go on its chain. Kiva knelt beside her with the slate.

It was a square brick shaft, eight feet deep. At the bottom the Ferren main went by, black, a yard wide. The water in it rose a finger on the brick, and fell. And across the shaft, a foot above the water, hung a plate of iron as broad as a door, held in two grooves in the brick like a window in its sash.

"That's a penstock," said Mara. Her voice came up hollow. "That's as pretty a sluice gate as I ever saw. It's hanging on a screw." The lamp swung. "And the screw goes up. It goes up through the brick, on a slant. Kiva. Where does that slant come out?"

Kiva looked along it. She did not need to measure.

"The post," she said.

They all looked at the post.

It was black, and split, and bound with two iron bands. It stood a pace out from its row, and it leaned.

"It isn't a post," said the man in the apron. He had gone round to the wall side, and had his thumbnail in the split. "There's iron in the middle of it. It's a wooden coat on a spindle. And that's no split, mistress. That's cut. That's a socket, square, a hand across, to take a bar." He looked up at the bottom wall, thirty feet of brick a yard and a half behind him. "That's why it's out of the row. Put a bar through a post in that row and it'd strike the wall on the first quarter turn."

"And the lean?"

"It's on the rake of the screw. You'd not get a straight spindle down to a gate that's eight feet under and a pace to one side." He patted the black wood, as a man pats a horse. "It was never crooked. It's aimed."

Somebody let out a breath behind them. It was Orla. She had been on her crate by the first shed since before the spade went in, with her stick between her boots.

She did not say anything. She got up, which took her some time, and came down the lane, and stood in front of the post as she might have stood in front of a person.

"One-and-twenty years," she said. "I've called you no help. In so many words. I said it as lately as the eighth month." The crooked face was doing something that Kiva had not seen it do. "I sat under a shut-off valve with my back to it, wondering what it was for, and told six hundred students that it wasn't in the manual."

"It wasn't," said Kiva.

"No. That's the worst of it. I was telling the truth." Orla laid the flat of her hand on the iron band. "I told Vane that I'd hold the lamp for his man. I'll hold it for a fitter. It comes to the same."

Mara had got up off her stomach. She stood at the edge of the shaft, wiping her left hand on her apron.

"I told a watch officer in the seventh month that every device in my house had a meter and a way to shut it down," she said. "I told my niece on Monday week that I'd found the meter for this, and not the other." She looked at the post, and at the hole where a cross had been. "A girl's been landing on it. Since the summer. I've been to that yard to watch her."

She began, rather helplessly, to laugh. Kiva had not heard her laugh like that since before the hall panel went dark.

"Well," said Mara, when she could. "*Two to the bar. Nine turns.* Let's see whether it remembers."

It did not remember.

They had a bar by ten, an ash pole as thick as a wrist, and four fitters on it, two to a side. They walked it round a quarter turn, and a half, and the screw groaned in the brick like a ship. At one full turn it stopped as though it had struck a wall.

"Rust," said the man in the apron. "And four hundred years of whatever the hill's washed into that slot. It wants oil, and it wants patience, and it wants somebody down that hole with a rake." He spat on his hands. "I can give you the oil."

Hollis Vane came at noon, as he had said that he would. He had the tin case under his arm. He looked at the open shaft, and at the bar standing through the post, and at four men in shirtsleeves leaning on it.

"I'm told that I owe a sponsor a lamp," he said.

"You owe me nothing," said Orla. "I'd no reason. I'd only a feeling about one. You said so."

"I did. And I gave it three days on the strength of a girl and a carry team. I'd like that written somewhere, since I shan't always be so sensible." He put the case down on her crate. "Rowan. What's it shut?"

Kiva had been waiting to be asked that. She had worked it out on the slate, with Mara, in the last hour.

"The hill from the street. Everything above this gate is the School's side. The drain that goes on along the lane, and the hill's rain, and one thing more." She pointed down the shaft. "There's a second mouth. A small one, in the north wall, above the gate. Mara had the lamp on it. It comes in from the core house."

"So that's how it would have reached your core," said Mara, to the keeper.

Ansty had come to look. She stood at the low wall with her keys.

"Round by the post," she said. "And I had a cork in my gully."

"If the gate's down, the breath stops here," said Kiva. "The core house never falls in. It can't; there's a door shut in the road. But it does nothing for the pumps. That's all below."

"And if it's shut, where does the hill's rain go?"

"Nowhere," said Mara. "It fills this yard to the top step in a wet night. So I shut it when I must, and not an hour sooner, and I open it when the bank's been seen to." She looked at the bar. "Supposing that it shuts at all."

"And if there's a thing in the street with an arm to put down?"

Mara did not answer at once.

"Every grating on Stopgate will be planked and bagged by tomorrow noon. Rusk's had carts out since six. The School's gully's done. That's Sorn's way, and it's a good one, and I'm taking it." She nodded at the shaft. "This is the one hole on the hill that I can't plank. I've a man going down it with a rake. It stays open until it's shut itself."

Vane looked at the hole for some time.

"Then I know where I'd go," he said, "if I were a thing that wanted water."

---

They filled twenty-six and twenty-seven on Tuesday at one, since there was no longer any reason for six hundred families to sit in the dusk.

Both drums went in before the fitter had finished counting sixty. Mara had her palm on the first. She took it away as one takes a hand off a stove.

"So that's my bank," she said. "Four-and-twenty fresh cans, and every one of them open for a slow sixty, in a house with the main going by under the floor. If the water's still, I've a chance. If it so much as rocks, I've filled them for nothing." She looked at her hand. "And every can in the Compact's store has had the hair in it since the thaw. They're halfway to listening before I've drawn the stopper. I want ash that keeps no time at all. A pinch, to salt each one. Something that's never heard a beat in its life."

"There's a shelf of it," said Kiva.

It was in the Wren House, at the end of the passage where the tins spent their night. Kiva had not been in that room since the spring. It ran from floor to ceiling, hundreds of small stoppered jars with a card round the neck of each, and Grader Halloran stood in front of it with her arms folded like a woman defending a larder.

"Plain," she said. "You want plain. So does everybody, this week. I've had the Halden technical office here, very civil, and I sent it away." She looked at Mara, and the work apron, and the license in its case. "You're the one who wrote *would mask trend* on a mast. I read the Works' circulars. What's it for?"

Mara told her. She did it in four sentences.

Halloran heard her out with her head a little on one side.

"Four-and-twenty," she said. "A pinch each. And you'll be back for thirty more, for your drums."

"The drums can wait. They're on open links; each of them's alone. If the bank's taken out and the water's quiet, I can do a drum a day through the winter with whatever old stock the wardens have in their cupboards. I'm told there's a woman on Weighbridge Street who can smell a cut."

"Cobb. She can. She's better than my comb." Halloran turned to the shelf. She ran her eye along a row of brass labels, low down, where the cards were brown. "Handful, plain. I've three hundred and six years of it. Every one's a pinch from a husk that somebody killed and somebody tinned, and wrote the day on. I can't card what I can't keep. I said that in a hearing room in the seventh month."

"I was there."

"Then you know that I'm not a generous woman." She took down a jar. She read the card, and then she read it aloud, since nobody in that building seemed able to do anything without a witness. "*Sag Line. The 4th of the third month, 2511. Plain. Held 106.* That's a hundred and sixty-nine years on a shelf to be put in a pump." She set it on the table. "Four-and-twenty. I'll choose them. I'll write each one off by its number, with the reason, and you'll sign under me. And you'll not have the thirty. Not if the Chair asks. A shelf that gives whenever it's asked nicely isn't a shelf."

"That's fair," said Mara.

"It's not fair. It's what's left when I've done being frightened." Halloran took down another.

Three of Seven had come to carry. Jude was at the door, and Anwen, and Perrin with a crate.

"There's plain nearer than that," said Jude.

He had not said it loudly. He was looking at the cold-room door across the passage, with its three seals.

"I'm only saying it since somebody will. We've two drams on our own book that came off a husk on the Sag in the fifth month, before any of this got into the ash. Ward-trace. No hair. It's sat under wax for five months." He scratched his jaw. "That's two hundred pinches, by my reckoning. It'd do the bank and the drums and leave some over. And there's six hundred drams in that room of the loudest ash in Meridian, if anybody wanted a thing for a string to walk to that wasn't a drain."

"No," said Anwen.

She said it without heat. She had the green book under her arm, and she did not open it.

"The first isn't salt. I had it written that it isn't training ash, in the seventh month, in case anybody was tempted. It's the same answer. It's medical. It's for somebody's hand, on a night that we haven't had yet, and when that night comes I'd like there to be something in the tin." Her level eyes went to Jude. "You're on the line by name. You've a right to say it. I'm glad that you did. I'd sooner it was said by one of us and refused than thought by all of us and left lying."

"And the second?"

"That's hers," said Anwen.

Kiva had known that it would come round to her. There was a bag in that room with her pencil on the tag.

"I wrote *Not for the ground,*" she said. "On two of them. I'd be the one saying that it was for the ground after all, since we'd found a use." She shook her head. "It's under three seals. One's the Works', and one's a street's, and Mrs. Cobb would walk up here in her overcoat and stand on the step. And I can't weigh what it would do. I'd be putting six hundred drams of a thing I don't understand on the cinders as bait for another thing I don't understand."

"It's evidence," said Halloran, without turning round. "I've a dram of it on this shelf, and a card that I've written four times and torn up. Nobody burns the only one of anything while I've the key."

"Then that's two things not spent," said Jude. He did not look put out. "I thought I'd ask. A man with a yield line gets to wondering what ash is for."

"It's for the next attempt," said Perrin. "That's what my dad says. He says it's the only thing it's ever bought anybody." He picked up the crate. "I'd like it known that I'm carrying three hundred years of history down a stair, and that if I trip, I'm going to keep walking and change my name."

---

Rhea Sorn sat in the Graywater watch room on Tuesday night with the map wall at her back, and ruled a line on a slate.

She had read that slate from the left for twenty-three years. *Closed. Lives. Works. Tinned. Remarks.* It was the whole of her trade in five words. One could not buy a column with the one to its right. She had told a boy so in the sixth month, and he had written it down as though it were news.

There was nothing to the left of *Closed.* There never had been. A call began when a bell rang, and what one did about it went into the first column, and what it had cost went into the others afterward.

*I cut, and I count after, and I'm thought very honest for it.*

She had said that to a girl with her hand in a cloth. She had been rather pleased with it at the time, as one is pleased with a thing that is true and costs nothing.

"You've been looking at that for a quarter of an hour," said Pike.

He was mending a hem by the stove. He did it every Tuesday, whether a hem wanted it or not.

"I've been thinking about Sunday."

"I thought that you might be." He bit off the thread. "You said it was too dear."

"It is too dear. I've seen what two of those gaps did to her. I've seen Stroud eat off a spoon." Rhea put the chalk down. "She's seventeen. She's on my card. If she goes up over that hole and comes down with a hand that only grips, that's mine, whoever read what off a slate. I'm not sorry that I said no."

"I'd not ask you to be."

"Then what is it, Pike?"

He folded the canvas over his knee. He took his time.

"You told her you'd never named a price first. I was by. I thought, that's handsome, that's a thing she'll keep." He looked up. "On Sunday you named one. Good. Then you paid it for her, in front of forty people, before she'd opened her mouth. That's not naming a price. That's the same thing you've always done, the other way up. You cut, and she counts after."

Rhea did not answer.

It was a hard thing to hear from a man who had knelt at her hems for the best part of her time on the van. It was harder that he had said it while threading a needle.

*What's there?* she asked herself. It was her oldest question.

A catcher, with eight fingers that reported. A healer's card that said he would name the price to a foreman's face. A sponsor who had stood at a rail and said, *I don't know that it will work.* A girl who had said, in a yard, in front of the Chair, *Sorn's right that it isn't hers to have offered,* and had then asked a sensible question about a cage.

*And what's behind me?*

She turned round, in her chair, and looked.

The map wall. A lamp for every mast in the district, and nine of them lit, since she had a student sitting under each with a drum and a boy to run. A painted table with her company second on it and a remark in the last column. And a long way behind that, a culvert on the Ferren in the autumn of '64, and a young blade who had gone in too far being hauled out of the wet by a Home Line woman who kept one hand shut on something at her throat.

Ilse Corran had not asked her whether she wanted pulling out. She had been glad of that for sixteen years.

She had been a casualty. The girl was not a casualty. She kept putting her in that column, and it was the wrong one.

There was a stick on the stair. It came up slowly, a step at a time.

"They said I'd find you," said Orla Dane.

She was out of breath. She stood in the door of the watch room in her old coat, and looked at the map wall, and did not ask leave to sit.

"We've never much cared for one another," she said. "I'll save you the trouble of being civil. I've come about my candidate."

"She's my attached catcher."

"She's both. That's what I've climbed your stair to say." Orla lowered herself onto the bench. "I'm her sponsor. If a hand's the price of a thing that she does, I sign for the hand. I've done it once and had it countersigned. You're her officer. You sign for the floor, and for who's seated on it, and you've every right to say that it isn't a floor for her." The stick turned in the old hands. "Neither of us signs for her. I thought once that I could. I'd a boy of seventeen and a sum worked out to the last figure, and I never asked him anything, since I knew the answers."

"I know about your boy."

"Everybody does. It's why I'm believed." She said it quite flatly. "I'm not asking you to say yes. I'd not, in your place; I don't know that I shall in my own. I'm asking you not to say it's settled. If that thing finds the one hole on the hill, somebody will turn round and look at her whether you forbid it or not. I'd like her to have been left something to say."

"And her aunt?"

"Her aunt drew a plan with a gap in it. She wrote *I can't draw this part* in the margin, in ink, and showed it to Rusk like that, sooner than draw the child into it." Orla almost smiled. "She's licensed. It's a wonderful thing in a guardian."

Rhea looked at the slate.

She took up the chalk. She ruled a sixth column, at the left of the first, a narrow one. She wrote a word over it.

*Before.*

"That's new," said Pike.

"It's a Home Line notion. Vane's got one in a ledger. I thought that it was a clerk's fancy when he read it off in the Pit." She turned the slate to the stove. "If it moors, and if there's no other way, and if she says so herself without being looked at. Then I hear the price from the healer, to my face. I hear from her what she does if it's refused. And I want to know who has every piece of it that isn't hers. Then I sign, or I don't." She put the chalk down. "It isn't a yes."

"I didn't climb two flights for a yes," said Orla. "I'd have distrusted one."

---

Kiva went to the Wren House infirmary that evening, at the hour when the lamps were turned down, and found that she had no speech at all.

It was a long room with twelve beds. Selka Dray had the one by the window. Her left leg lay outside the blanket in a board, from the hip to the heel, and she was reading by a shaded lamp with a pencil in her teeth.

She took the pencil out.

"You," she said.

"Me."

"If it's about my rule, I've written to the Chair already. I'll not unwrite it."

"It isn't. I want to ask you for something." Kiva stood at the foot of the bed. There was a chair, and she had not been invited to it. "I'll say all of it, and then what I do if you say no."

"You've got that off Pryce. Half the Circuit's doing it." The grey eyes were sunk, and perfectly sharp. "Go on."

"There's a thing that may come up in the street below the School. A string, or something like one. If it does, it'll put an arm down into the drain, and the arm will haul and ease on a count, as that cord did on your leg. If somebody's to take the arm off the water, they'll want the slack called. To the half count." She kept her hands still. "I can't call it. I tried on Sunday and I nipped a first-year's fingers. I've one hand that hears and one that doesn't, and I took the middle."

"I heard. Marten told me. He said she folded like a conjuror."

"Brask called it after that. He's good. But if it comes to the real thing, he's wanted on a bar, with Cade; there's a gate to be shut, and it's two still-doors' work." She drew a breath. "You had it across your leg for the best part of a quarter of an hour. You counted it. Eight to a breath, and it bites on the first four. You told me so lying on your back. I don't know anybody in Meridian who's been as near it for as long and kept the count."

Dray said nothing.

"I'm not seated for it. Sorn's said no, and it isn't to be asked of me, and nobody's asking. So this may be for nothing whatever. You'd be carried up a hill in a chair in the cold, to sit and watch planks." Kiva made herself finish. "And if you say no, it's Brask if he can be spared, and Wace if he can't. Wace has a good ear. He's never had it on him."

There was a long silence. Somewhere down the ward somebody coughed.

"You've not said that I owe you," said Selka Dray.

"You don't."

"You took a thing off my leg."

"That was mine to spend. You told me not to make a speech."

"So I did." Dray put the book down, open, on the blanket. Kiva saw that it was the Circuit's rules, and that the margins were full of pencil. "I'll tell you what I've been doing for seventeen days. I've been lying here with a blade arm and no leg, being of no use to anybody, and I find that I mind that a good deal more than I mind you. I'd not have thought it possible."

"Nor would I."

"Don't be pleased. It's not a compliment." Her jaw moved. "I'll do it. I've terms."

"Go on."

"There's a needle on you. A mast, in that yard, and Bexley at it with his flags. I'll not call a count for anything of yours that isn't being read. If the red goes up, I stop, in the middle of a word." She held up a second finger, exactly as Jude had once. "Marten carries the chair. With a porter. He'll not let anybody else, and I'll not have him argued with."

"That's two."

"The third is that it's written. That I objected to you twice, and that I think what I thought, and that I'm counting since I'm the one who can and for no other reason. I'd not have anybody suppose that I've come round."

"I'll write it tonight. I'll show you the page."

"You'll not. I'll take your word. I've noticed that it's good." Dray picked up her book again. "It bites on the first of the four. Not all four alike. The first is the worst, and then it only holds. I'd call *in* a hair before it lets go, since you'll want the time to move your hands. You'd have learned that if you'd lain under it."

"I'd not have liked to."

"No," said Selka Dray. "I didn't much."

Kiva went down the infirmary stair with her right hand on the rail.

She wrote it that night, on the right, where the reasons went. She put the three terms down as they had been said. Then she sat with the pencil for a while, and under them she wrote a thing that she had been not writing since Sunday.

*Sorn said no. I was relieved. Then I wasn't. I want it to be me. That isn't a reason; it's only what's so. If it comes, I'm to say what it costs, and what I do if it's refused, and who has the parts that aren't mine. I've three of those now. The cord. The count. The corner, after six.*

She looked at the last line. Brask had said yes to that one at the Hall on Monday, with his notebook open, as though she had asked him to hold a ladder.

*And if Brask is kept at the bar: Wace. Asked. He said, "I've been a post before."*

It did not look like being ready. It looked like a sheet with a great many corners, and somebody else's name on most of them.

---

Four of them signed the book on Wednesday at five, standing up, on the lid of the Low Lane's quench box.

It was a short entry. Anwen read it.

"*Wednesday the 5th. Three-door, K. Rowan: 0.59, from held. One go. Only on a foreman's direction, under the officer's hand, with the price said first by her healer. Cade keeps the word. If not directed, returned to held, whole.*" She looked up. "*Why:* I've left it bare. I'll fill it on the night, or I'll rule it through."

"That's a dram of mine that I didn't expect to see again," said Perrin. He signed. "I said *no* to this in the seventh month. I said *yes, with a brake* in the eighth. I'd like it known that tonight I've not the least idea what I think, and I'm signing so that there's something to say *off* to."

"Held, thirteen and fifty-three hundredths," said Kellan. He blotted it. "I've had the others' call drams out of the press. They're combed."

The fitters had the gate at seven turns by then.

Kiva had been to look. The bar stood through the black post, and four men in shirtsleeves sat on the ground beside it with their backs against the wall. Down the shaft, on a ladder, a boy with a rake was bringing up grey silt in a bucket.

"It's the slot," said the man in the apron. "The last two turns, the gate's to go down into its groove in the floor of the drain, and the groove's full to the brim. He's been at it since noon. I'd say midnight. I'd not say it loudly."

Drum twenty-nine fell in at twenty to eight.

It went as the others had. There was a crowd at its rail, and a needle that stood, and trembled, and went up without any hurry at all. Nobody cheered and nobody cried out. They had seen it before. A warden rang her handbell once, and people on the top stretch of Stopgate came to their doors and stood there with their coats on.

"Friday for the core house," said Mara. "By the float. I shall have my gate down by then if I've to go down that ladder with a spoon." She had the plan under her arm, and forty names on a list. "And the bank on Thursday night at one, when the sump's lowest. Every operator that Rusk has, and eleven students who stood a Bench. I've told them to sleep tomorrow afternoon. I don't suppose that one of them will."

The planks were down. Kiva had walked the hill at dusk to see them. Every grating between the tram rails had three thick boards across it and a row of sandbags on the boards, forty of them, from the Brickfields road to the black gate. It looked like a street that was expecting a flood.

She was in the bottom yard at eleven. She was not on any duty. She had gone to see whether the boy with the rake had come up.

He had not. There was a lamp down the shaft still, and the scrape of iron on brick.

She was standing at the gap in the low wall, with her hand on the cold stone, when she felt it change.

It did not grow. She would have said, afterward, that it turned over. The slow even push that she had had under her palm for nine days had been a thing going by. It was not going by any longer. It had stopped, somewhere below her on the hill, and was standing still, and getting deeper, as water deepens behind a hand.

"Noa," she said. And then, as loudly as she had ever said anything, up at the black bulk of the core house roof: "*Noa! What's the drum doing?*"

There was a pause of perhaps two counts.

The School's bell rang.

It rang two and two and two, over her head, and it did not stop. And through it came a thin cracked voice that she would have known anywhere.

"*It's the drum! It's the drum, it isn't the mark! It's climbing! It's the drum!*"

She ran to the wicket.

She had no business there. She was recovery, and dark, and seventeen. But the wicket stood open, and there was nobody in it, and she looked down Stopgate.

It was a long straight hill, wet, with a lamp at every twentieth pace. Halfway down it the lamps had a different color to light.

It lay in the crown of the road. It was between the tram rails, where the planks were, and it ran from the rail of the twenty-eighth drum house up to the rail of the twenty-ninth, sixty paces and more. It was thin. It was blue.

It was not a stroke standing in the air, like the gap at the edge of a door. It was lying down. It was a seam, a hand wide, in the surface of the street, as though somebody had drawn a knife along a tablecloth and the color of the table were showing through.

The planks over the nearest grating had gone white. She saw frost run along them, and round the sandbags, like spilled milk.

Then, the whole length of that seam, things began to come up.

They did not come one above another. She had seen a string climb out of a hatch a knot at a time. These rose together. Eight grey shoulders came up through the blue, side by side, eight paces apart, each on a pair of legs as thick as lampposts, and between each and the next there ran a white cord at the height of a man's chest.

It was a string. It was a string laid on its side.

There was not a top to it. Every one of the eight had a blunt head of its own, and every head was turning, slowly, a quarter of a circle, as a weathercock turns.

Below her, on the hill, eleven handbells went off like a dropped tray.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/movement-006-pre-repair/chapter-45.md

# Chapter 45 — Kiva: Paid Out

Mara came up the ladder out of the sluice shaft with silt to the elbows, and she had heard the bell.

"Is it the mark?"

"It's the drum. Noa says so. I've seen it." Kiva had run back across the cobbles. She was out of breath, and her knee was telling her about it. "It's in the road. Between twenty-eight and twenty-nine. It's a tear, lying down, and there's a string in it laid on its side. Eight. Every one of them's got a head."

Mara stood quite still, with her hands held away from her apron.

"It's a night early," she said. "I've a bank to take out, and my forty are in their beds." She looked down the shaft, where a lamp still burned and a rake still scraped. "And I've a gate that wants two turns."

"The fitters have the bar."

"The fitters have had the bar since Tuesday." She wiped her left hand, the reading one, on the only clean corner of her apron, with great care. "Listen to me, since I shan't be here. That gate's to come down the moment the slot's clear, and if four men can't shift it, it's two still-doors. Cade and Brask. You know that. You know who has what better than anybody on this hill; you've been writing it down for a week. Say it aloud, to whoever's standing nearest. Don't suppose that they know."

"Where will you be?"

"The pump house. There's a Works van at the foot of the hill. If I'm to quench four-and-twenty cells tonight, I must find four-and-twenty people to do it with, and I've a list in my pocket of where they sleep." She was already unpinning the apron. "I'll be on the municipal wire. Mrs. Ansty has a key in her lodge and a clerk who can work it."

She stopped, with the apron in her hands.

"I'll say one thing more, and I'll not say it twice. I shan't be on this hill. I'm glad of it, and I'm ashamed of being glad." Her voice was quite level. "Whatever's asked of you tonight, or not asked, I shan't be at a rail with my hand on my coat. It won't be me who looks at you. I told you in the spring that I wanted you safe without owning what you chose. I find that I can only manage it from two miles off."

"Mara—"

"Don't. I've a bank." She put the apron into Kiva's arms, as she had once put a book there. "Mind that boy on the ladder. He's fourteen, and he thinks that a bell is for other people."

She went out by the wicket, walking fast.

---

Hollis Vane had the hill by ten minutes past eleven, and he took it as he took everything, at a walk.

"This is a response," he said. He stood in the black gateway with his slate, and his outdoor voice went down Stopgate and across the yard behind him. "It's mine. Wardens, your bells, and your red handles; I want every box on this hill dark before its door's shut. Bottom forty, down. Top two-and-twenty, up, by the lane. Carry, you've walked it. You know where the post is."

"*Post, left!*" said somebody on the hill, who had not been asked. "*Keep right!*"

"There," said Vane. "It's got about."

The litters began to come.

It was not Saturday. Kiva knew that in the first minute. On Saturday there had been laughter on the hill, and a child who had not been told to be quiet. Tonight there was only the sound of feet, and doors, and a handbell going steadily along the top stretch like a pulse.

She was at the gap in the low wall. It was her place, and she had been put there twice. Pru stood beside her with the shuttered lamp.

"Through here. Lane's ahead. *Post, left, keep right.* Say it back."

"Post left, keep right."

They said it back. Every team said it. Pettit came through at a trot with a real woman on his poles, a very old one, wrapped to the chin, and he bawled it over his shoulder with the fervor of a convert. The call ran ahead of him down the dark lane, and came back from the sheds in Healer Aske's dry voice, where the Wren House cart stood at the side gate with its tailboard down.

Nobody struck the post.

There was a bar through it. Four fitters stood at the bar in their shirtsleeves, not turning it, since there was a boy down the shaft at their feet and one does not turn a screw over a boy. They had moved the litter road a yard to the north with a strip of white tape.

"How long?" said Kiva, to the man in the apron.

"He's got the east groove clear. He's on the west." The man did not take his eyes from the hole. "A quarter of an hour. I said midnight. I'd be glad to be made a liar."

A van came up the hill.

She heard it before she saw it, the whine of its rings on the wet setts. It stopped below the twenty-eighth drum house, where the blue began. Slate blue coats got down from it, and a square shape with a roll of canvas on each shoulder, and a tall woman with a white streak in her hair, who stood for a moment in the road with her hands in her pockets, reading.

"Rowan," said Vane. He had not turned. "You read ground. You've a first-year who can stand at a gap and say four words. Give her the gap. Come to this gate and tell me what that thing does at the top. I can't see the top and the bottom both."

"Pru," said Kiva. "The gap. Yours. All of it."

"Mine," said Pru. Her voice hardly shook.

Kiva went to the gate.

---

From the black gateway one could see the whole of it.

It stood in the crown of the road, between the rails, eight grey shapes abreast down sixty paces of hill. Each was the size of a Flask's knot, as big as a barrel, on a pair of thick legs. The white cord ran from shoulder to shoulder, and from the cord a wall of frost hung down to the setts and stood above it, thin as breath on a window. It cut Stopgate in two the long way.

Every head was turning. They did not turn together. That was what made her skin creep. Eight blunt tops went round a quarter, and back, each in its own time, like eight people in a dark room listening for the same sound.

"Count them from the top," said Vane. "The one nearest us is One."

"One's at the rail of twenty-nine. Eight's at twenty-eight. They're eight paces apart." She had her book on her forearm. "They're seeking. They've not found it. The planks are white, under every one, and nothing's gone down."

"Sorn's way. Good." He raised his voice. "*Sorn! It's a string on a street! It's yours!*"

Down the hill four wards had brought a gate up, twelve paces below the eighth knot, as bright as a shopfront.

Eight heads went past it.

"I didn't suppose that they'd care for it," said Rhea Sorn. Her voice came up the hill quite clearly. She had not raised it. "Leave it lit. It's the rule, and it'll do for the husks. Pike. Your cloth, under Eight. I'm taking it from the bottom."

Kiva watched her do it.

She had seen that cut before, at a weir, at first light. Rhea Sorn had no Stride. She did not go up the back of anything. She walked to the lowest of the eight as a woman walks to a door, and stopped at an arm's length, and waited, with her blade arm down.

"*Not yet,*" said Pike, from the hem. "It's turning. *Now.*"

The light was yellow, and level, and it lasted most of a second.

The eighth knot fell in through its own shoulders. Sixty drams came down on the canvas like sand out of a sack, and lay.

"*Dark! One.*"

The cord came off it. It whipped back up the hill, the loose end of it, and the seventh knot took it in at the shoulder as a reel takes a line. There was no gap. There were seven, with a cord from each to the next, and a foot of blue seam lying open in the road where the eighth had been.

"Sealed," said Pike. "Fifty-two." He had his palm on the neck. "It's loud. It's as loud as anything I had off the Pit. Red."

"Then that's how it's done," said Rhea. "It's slow. I've done slow. Seven."

"Sir," said Kiva.

She had not been watching the bottom. She had been told to watch the top.

At the rail of the twenty-ninth drum house, eight paces above the first knot, the blue line had moved. It had run on up the hill, in the crown of the road, as a crack runs in ice. And at the end of it something was coming up through the setts. It had shoulders.

"There's another," she said. "At the top. It came up as she cut. There's eight again."

Vane turned his head.

He looked for a long moment. Down the hill Rhea Sorn was walking to the seventh knot. Pike had his second cloth down.

"*Now.*"

The yellow light. The sound of sand.

And at the top, the blue ran on another eight paces, toward the wall, and another pair of shoulders rose out of the road.

"It's not growing," said a voice at Kiva's elbow. "It's being paid out."

It was Lowen Brask. He had a litter pole in one hand; he had just come up from the lane. His square face was the color of the wall.

"That's a hold," he said. "I said so in the Pit and I'll say it here. There's a tear, and it's kept open, and what's in it comes through for as long as it's kept. Only there's nobody keeping it. There's no square." He looked down at the wet setts between his boots. "It's the drain. The whole main's hauling and easing under that seam, on a four and a four. I stood a summer doing that with my shoulders. The road's doing it by itself."

"So she can cut at the bottom until morning," said Vane.

"And it'll walk to the top while she does. She's bailing."

Vane wrote on his slate. He wrote very little.

"Bexley's on the roof. There's a clerk on the key in the lodge." He tore the corner off a sheet. "*To M. Rowan, the Ferren pump house. It is a held tear. The main is holding it. Your bank tonight, as soon as you have the hands, and the pumps stopped. The holes are planked; it has no arm down. H.V.*" He gave it to a runner. "That's the Works' part, and I'm glad of it. Sorn! Hold your cut! You're feeding it!"

Rhea had stopped already. She stood below the sixth knot with her blade dark, looking up the hill, and Kiva saw her count.

"Eight," she said. "I've cut two and there are eight." She put her hands back in her pockets. "Then I'm a gate and a pair of eyes until somebody stops that water. I don't like it. I've not liked anything since the thaw."

The ninth knot's head had begun to turn.

It did not turn toward the gate. It turned east, slowly, a quarter of a circle, and stopped. It was pointing at a house.

It was a tall house, near the top, with brass on the door. There was a light behind the fanlight, small and steady and yellow.

"That's a box," said Kiva. "Somebody's not pulled their handle."

---

Anwen Pryce had been given the end of the wall again, and this time the wall was walking.

That was Mace's notion. He had stood behind his line at the foot of the blue for one slow minute, looking at boots, and then he had said, in the voice in which he asked for the salt, that a gate was no use to a thing that would not look at it. What was wanted was a porch.

"Every house on this hill has a box in it," he had said. "A box is a lamp. Until the handle's pulled, it's the brightest thing that string can read, and it's ten paces from a front door with people behind it. So we stand between. A wall along the curb, from the bottom to the top, and it goes up the hill as the wardens do. Ten of you won't cover sixty paces. You'll cover the house that's lit."

Ten of them made forty paces of silver, cart-wide each, along the east curb. Six were the night van's. Four were students. Anwen had the uphill end, with Tibb on her left hand and the night on her right.

*If it dips, I'll hear it.*

She had learned an end in a culvert, standing still. She had not learned one that moved. A wall that walked had to be let go at the back and brought up at the front, a holder at a time, each coming up inside the next as a relief comes. It was Graywater's oldest trick. It was only that nobody did it for a hundred paces up a hill.

"Fifty-two's dark," said a warden's voice, behind her. "Fifty-four. Dark. Fifty-six, there's nobody in. Dark."

"Step," said Anwen. "Tibb. I'm coming off. Now."

She let go of nothing. That was the craft of it. She leaned on Tibb's wall until hers held no weight at all, and then it was not there, and she had gone three paces up the wet pavement and brought it up again, new, at the top.

Six paces to her right, across the gutter, a frost wall stood twice her height. She could feel the cold of it on her cheek. Above it the cord ran white from one grey shoulder to the next, drawing up and letting down.

She did not look at it. She looked at the curb.

"Fifty-eight," said the warden. Her voice changed. "Fifty-eight's lit. Mr. Garland. *Mr. Garland!* Your handle!"

There was a light behind the fanlight. Anwen saw it at the edge of her eye, small and yellow.

There was a head turning, too. It was the ninth, the new one, eight paces up the hill from where the wall ended. It came round east and stopped.

"That's on us," said Tibb. He had stopped humming. "Pryce. That's yours. It's past the end."

"I know where it is."

It was eight paces beyond her. There was nobody to bring a wall up inside. There was only the pavement, and a tall house with brass on the door, and a grey thing in the road that had found the one lamp on the hill.

The cord swung.

It did not come fast. The ninth knot leaned, as the Flask had leaned at the Pit, and the white line between it and the next below bellied out toward the east curb like a rope with a weight hung on it. The frost came with it. It crossed the gutter.

"*Step!*" said Anwen. "All of it! Eight paces! I'm going long!"

She went.

She let go of Tibb's wall without waiting to feel it take, which was a thing that she had been told for four weeks never to do. She ran eight paces up the wet stone and stood in front of a green door with her back to the brass, and put up her hands.

The wall came. It was hers alone, a cart wide, with nothing on its left and nothing on its right.

The frost met it.

It did not strike. It leaned. It was the weight of a string's knot, sixty drams with legs under it, and it came onto her two hands as a full tin comes onto a shelf. She had held that weight before, with three beside her, for forty minutes. She had never held it with nobody.

*It'll bear weight.* She had said that about a different thing. She found that it was true of this one.

"I've an end with no middle," she said. She said it in a carrying voice, level, as one reports a tin. "Fifty-eight. I'm holding. I want a neighbor on my left and I want that handle pulled. In that order."

"Coming up," said Tibb. Then, sharply: "Stiles. You're short. You're a hand short on the outside, and she's got nothing to lean on if you join like that."

"So I am," said Stiles.

She felt them come. It was like hearing a cart turn into the street. Tibb's wall came up inside her left edge, and beyond his, Stiles's, snug this time, and the weight on her two hands became a weight on six. She did not dip. She knew that she had not, though there was nobody to tell her.

The green door opened behind her.

"You're being rescued," said a voice that she had known since it was a first-year's with purple hands. "It's on a count. There's a lady holding your porch up, so we're to be quick."

"I'll not leave Admiral," said an old man.

"He's coming. He's got his own handle. Brask, the box."

There was a sound from inside the house, a flat iron *clack.* The light in the fanlight went out.

Anwen felt the weight go off her hands as one feels a hand lifted from a shoulder. The ninth head turned away. It went round west, and north, and stopped pointing at nothing, and the cord drew back across the gutter to the crown of the road.

"Dark," said the warden. "Fifty-eight. And about time."

They came out past her. Perrin had the chair in his arms, with an old man in it and a cage on the old man's knee. Lowen Brask walked behind with a hand on the back rail. A bird was saying something disagreeable.

"I'd like it known," said Perrin, as he went by, without turning his head, "that I mind every one of these. That's Pryce. She's held a string on her own outside a front door, and I've had my back to it. Again."

"Walk," said Anwen.

She did not laugh. She found afterward that she had wanted to.

Mace was behind her. She had not heard him come.

He was looking at her boots. He looked at them for some time. Then he looked along the wall, at the place eight paces down the hill where she had let go of a join without waiting for it.

"You came off Tibb before he had you," he said.

"Yes."

"I've a card in a drawer that says you never do that."

"I know what it says."

"Why did you?"

She had the answer. It was not a tidy one.

"Because there was a door with people behind it, and the end of the wall was in the wrong place. I'd have been right about the join and they'd have had a hem through the fanlight." She kept her eyes on the curb. "I said so as I went. *All of it. Eight paces. I'm going long.* If I'd gone silent, I'd have been wrong."

"Hm," said Mace.

He wrote on his board. He did not show her.

"Step," he said. "It's not done. There's an eleventh coming up, and the sixties are lit."

She stepped.

---

The head of it reached the wall at half past eleven.

Kiva had watched it come. She had called every one to Vane as it stood up, in her carrying voice, with the number. *Ten, sixteen paces above twenty-nine. Eleven, at the last grating. The planks are holding.* She had written the minute beside each.

The blue line ran up the crown of Stopgate like a crack in a plate. It came to the stone sill of the black gate, and there it stopped.

It did not come through. She saw that. Outside the gate the main lay shallow, a yard under the setts, with a grating over it every twenty paces. Inside, it dived. It went down eight feet to a brick barrel, and the blue would not follow it there. It ended at the sill, clean, as a crack ends at the edge of a tile.

The twelfth knot came up in the gateway.

She had gone back from it already. Vane had her by the sleeve, and they stood on the cobbles, ten paces inside, and looked at a grey thing as big as a barrel standing under the School's device with frost running up the oak on either hand.

"Twelve," she said. Her voice was not steady. "In the gate. That's all the road it's got."

"Keeper!" said Vane.

"I've seen it," said Ansty.

She was at the iron door of the core house. She had not hurried. She had the pin out of a red lever already, and she held it up in two fingers so that the watch officer could see it had been drawn.

"That's a thing that reads," she said. "And I've the biggest lamp on the hill behind this door. I said that I'd pull when it was at my wall. It's at my wall."

She pulled.

There was a sound from inside the square brick house, deep and rattling, felt through the cobbles more than heard. It was a weight falling a long way, on purpose.

And the hum stopped.

Kiva had lived inside that hum for six months. It had come through the Low Lane's wall all day, like a kettle thinking about boiling, and she had long ago ceased to hear it. She heard it stop. It was like a held breath let go. All over the upper school, the windows that had been dim went brown.

"Rods down," said Ansty. She put the pin in her pocket. "Core's safe. The School's on its boxes, and I'll have every one of those pulled inside ten minutes. There's nothing in my house now for it to want."

The twelfth head was turning.

It went past the core house. It went past the Low Lane, and the gully with its planks and sandbags. It came round west, along the foot of the bottom wall, toward a gap in a low wall and a strip of packed dirt beyond it.

It stopped.

*I know where I'd go,* a watch officer had said, on Tuesday, *if I were a thing that wanted water.*

"It's the shaft," said Kiva.

She was already running.

"*The lid!*" She had never shouted so loudly in her life. "*Get the lid on! It's found the hole! Is that boy up?*"

He was not up.

She came through the gap with her knee screaming. Pru was there, flat against the wall, with the lamp. The four fitters were at the bar. And down the square brick shaft, at the foot of the leaning post, a lamp still burned, and a rake still scraped on iron.

"*Ned!*" The man in the apron was on his knees at the edge. "Up! Leave the rake! Up the ladder, now!"

"There's a hand's breadth left in the west groove—"

"*Now!*"

Something was coming along the wall.

It came from the gateway, at the height of a man's chest. It was white, and as thick as a wrist, and it did not hang from anything. It reached. It came along the foot of the brick as a hawser is paid out along a quay, drawing up and letting down, and where it passed, the wall went grey with frost.

It was an arm, three-and-twenty paces long, and it was feeling for a hole.

"Cade!" said Kiva.

She did not know where he was. She said it as one says a role on a floor.

"*Carry! The shaft! There's a boy on a ladder!*"

He came out of the dark of the lane like a dray.

He had been bringing an empty litter back for the next house. He let go of the poles. He did not ask what, or where. He went down on his chest at the edge of the shaft, with his legs across the white tape, and put one long arm into the hole.

"You're being rescued," said Perrin, into the dark. "Don't argue. I've had an old man argue. Take my wrist. Not the ladder. *Me.*"

There was a scrabble. A small hand came up into the lamplight and shut on his forearm.

He lifted.

He did not haul. He stood up. He got one knee under him, and then the other foot, and he rose off the packed dirt with a boy of fourteen hanging from one arm like a bucket from a crane, and swung him clear, and set him down against the shed wall four paces off.

"There," he said. "That's one that I saw."

"The lid," said the man in the apron. "Bars! Get it over!"

Three fitters had the iron by its ring. It was a yard square, and it had taken three of them to lift it out on Tuesday with daylight and all morning. They dragged it. It came across the dirt a hand at a time, grating.

The arm came through the gap.

Kiva was six paces from it. She had her back against the first post of the north row, and she could not have moved.

It did not hurry. It went by her at chest height, humming a little, and the cold came off it like the cold off a cellar door. It found the leaning post. It went round the foot of it, on the lane side, and tipped, as water tips over a sill.

The lid was half across.

The arm went down beside it. It went down through the gap that was left, a foot wide, into eight feet of brick shaft, and she heard it meet the water.

It was a small sound. It was the sound of a rope's end dropped into a well.

For a moment nothing happened.

Then the cord drew up, a hand. All along the foot of the bottom wall, and through the black gate, and down seventy paces of wet hill, ten grey shapes leaned back a little, together. And eased.

In. And out.

"It's in the drain," said the man in the apron. He had let go of the ring. "It's got its arm down my slot."

Kiva put her right palm flat against the post behind her.

She could feel it. She did not need the ground. It came up through the old wood, slow and even and many, as it had come up through a doorpost on the Brickfields road. It was stronger than it had been. It was a great deal stronger.

It was not going by any longer. It was being driven.

"Vane," she said.

He was in the gap. He had seen it go down.

"Say it."

"It's pumping. The main. It's the same as the trough." She took her hand off the wood. "If my aunt stops her pumps now, the water won't lie still. She can't fill. And if Sorn cuts, it runs down that arm into the drain, and it's under the pump house in an hour."

"And the gate?"

"It can't come down. There's a thing in the slot."

Vane did not write that. He stood and looked at a white cord going down into the ground at the foot of a post.

The runner came through the gap behind him. It was a School porter, with a slip of paper off the key in the keeper's lodge, and he was holding it out in front of him as though it were hot.

Vane read it aloud. He read everything aloud.

"*Pump house, eighteen minutes to twelve. I have nineteen hands of twenty-four and the rest coming. Pumps stopped two minutes, on trial. The water is still moving.
