It moved less a quarter of an hour ago. Something is in it. Pumps running again. I cannot fill over this. Tell me when it is out. M. Rowan.*"

He folded the slip.

"*Tell me when it is out,*" he said.

The yard was very quiet. There were perhaps twenty people in it now. The fitters stood at their bar. Lowen Brask had come to the gap, and Rhea Sorn behind him, with her coat open and her hands out of her pockets. Orla was on her crate by the first shed, where she had been since the bell. A tall grey figure stood at the side gate, by the Wren House cart, and beside her a man with a pin in his lapel.

Somebody was setting up a tripod under the shed lamp. It was Noa. He had come down off the roof with the travelling mast on his back, and nobody had told him to.

Kiva looked at the cord.

It lay in the angle of the post, where it left the ground, drawing up and letting down. It had a slack in it. Four counts of haul, and four of ease. She knew what it weighed on the slack. She had held one like it in a tray between her palms.

She knew what was four paces from it. She had not looked yet. She would have to put a door down and find out.

Nobody in the yard looked at her.

She saw them not do it. She saw a watch officer study his slate, and a Torch study the wall. She saw the Placement Chair fold her hands. They had every one of them heard it said on a blue brick floor, and they were keeping to it as people keep to the edge of a pavement.

*It is not to be asked of her.*

It was the kindest thing that anybody had ever written about her. She understood, standing there with her back to a post, that it had a second half which nobody had been able to write. If she was not to be asked, then nobody could say it for her. Not her sponsor, and not her officer, and not an aunt two miles away with nineteen hands.

It would have to be said by the one person in the yard who would rather have been told.

Her mouth was dry. She had an answer, and for once it was not standing ready. She had to go and fetch it, a piece at a time.

"I'd like to say what it costs," said Kiva.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/movement-006-pre-repair/chapter-46.md

# Chapter 46 — Kiva: Call the Turn

"Then say it," said Hollis Vane.

He had not looked surprised. He had his chalk in his hand already.

"I'd like it in the order that Sorn asked for." Kiva kept her back against the post. It was something to stand against. "What it is. What it costs. What I do if it's refused. And who has every piece of it that isn't mine."

Rhea Sorn's head came round.

"Who told you that I'd asked for an order?"

"Orla. On the tram. She said that it wasn't a yes."

"It wasn't." The pale eyes rested on her. "Go on. I'm listening for the last part hardest."

"That arm's a hem under load. It's got a slack; four hauling and four easing. I stand at the hole. I put a ward under it for one tap, on the slack, and it comes off the water. It throws me. I fold, and I go with the throw four paces, and I put it down in dry ground and stand on it. Hand, Ember, still." She heard her own voice, thin in the cold yard. "Then it's hauling on me and not on the drain. The slot's clear. The gate can come down behind it. And there's nothing driving the main, so my aunt can stop her pumps and have the water lie."

"For how long?" said Vane.

"I held a corner for ten in the Pit. That was three of them. This is ten, on a longer arm. I'll not hold it. That's the first piece that isn't mine." She drew a breath. "I plant it. On six, it goes to somebody who can stand under it, and I shut. That was to be Brask."

"It still is," said Lowen Brask, from the gap.

"Not if the bar wants you. Four fitters have had that gate since Tuesday, and it's stuck at seven. If the last two turns are still-doors' work, that's you and Cade. *Two to the bar.* Then the corner's Wace's, with Moss at his other end. A plank. I asked them on Monday." She made herself say it slowly, as she would have called a mark. "I'm saying it now, before, so that if it turns, it's a turn that's been called."

"The count," said Rhea.

"Not mine. I nipped a first-year on Sunday. Brask's, if he's free. If he's at the bar—"

"Then it's mine," said a voice from the lane.

They had come in by the side gate. Kiva had not heard them for the bell. There was a thin boy in Wren House green, and a porter, and between them a wooden chair with a rail, such as the infirmary used on its stairs. A red head was in it. A leg in a board stuck out in front like a bowsprit.

"I heard that bell from my bed," said Selka Dray. She was the color of tallow, and she had a blanket round her shoulders. "I said that I'd come. I'd terms. There's a needle on her; I can see Bexley's tripod. Put me down by that post, Marten, where I can see the hole. Not so near. I've been near."

They put her down against the eighth post of the north row, five paces from the shaft.

"The cord on Seven's rung is Finch's, on her own cloth," said Kiva. "The wax is Pryce's. The word's Cade's; he may say *off* from the bar, and Orla may, and I may, in two places." She had come to the end of it. It was a long list. There was hardly anything left on it with her name. "That's who has what. I've only the middle."

"The price," said Rhea. "I'll hear that from him."

Healer Aske had walked down the lane. He had no coat on. He took her left hand without a word and turned it to Pru's lamp, and pressed the palm, and the wrist, and looked at the nails.

"No white," he said. "I looked for it. I'd have been glad of it." He did not let go. "I'll say this to the foreman's face and to the officer's, since that's what I wrote on a card. I don't know. The two fingers paid the last time, since they'd been hurt first. They've nothing left. So it comes out of the next thing, and the next thing's the hand. I think it goes to the elbow."

"And comes back?" said Vane.

"I think so. I've no case to think it from. Nobody has done this three times and let a healer watch." His dry voice did not rise. "It may be that she keeps a hand that grips and doesn't feel. Like her aunt's right, only the whole of it. That's the price as near as I can say it, and I'll not round it down to be obliging."

Nobody spoke. Along the foot of the wall the white cord drew up, and let down.

"And if I say no?" said Rhea.

"Then you cut from the bottom, and it's paid out at the top, and it stands on this hill with its arm in the main." Kiva had that ready. It was not a good answer, and she had made it on purpose. "The pumps go on running in step. My aunt can't fill over moving water. It's as it was this morning, with a string added, until a cell fouls or somebody thinks of a thing that I haven't. I'd not blame you. I was glad on Sunday when you said it."

"Were you?"

"For about an hour."

Rhea took a slate from inside her coat. It was not Vane's. It had six columns on it, and the sixth was narrow, and ruled at the left.

"Did anybody look at you?" she said. "Just now. Before you spoke."

"No. I watched you all not."

"Hm." The chalk moved. She read it out as she wrote it, which Kiva had never heard her do. "*Before: the left hand, to the elbow. Her healer does not know that it returns. Offered. Not asked.*" She looked up. "Under my hand, then. I've said for twenty-three years that I count after. I find that I've a column for the other."

"I sign for the hand," said Orla.

She had come down from her crate. She stood with both palms on the head of her stick.

"As her sponsor. I've seen it clean twice. I've seen it missed three times. It isn't on her paper and I'll not put it there." She did not look at Kiva. She looked at the cord. "And I don't know that it will work. I said that at the Pit and I was ashamed of how new it felt. It's a little less new."

"I am a witness," said Ysra Crest, from the shed. "I have a page with one line on it. Mr. Bexley has two flags."

"I've the pen on her," said Noa. His voice cracked. "I've had it on her since she put her hand to that post."

Vane wrote.

He wrote for longer than he had in the Pit. He held the slate up to the lamp.

"*Direction to K. Rowan, catcher, T7, attached, under R. Sorn's hand. Three doors in sequence, hand, Ember, still, declared. Cost, as stated before by her healer: the left hand, to the elbow; he does not know that it returns. Reason: an arm of a string of ten in the Stopgate sluice, driving the Ferren main, and a bank that cannot be filled while it drives. No other hand on this hill takes it off the water. Offered by the candidate. Not asked. The count is S. Dray's. The corner after six is L. Brask's, or Wace's.*" He lowered it. "Do you take it?"

There was a thing to be done first. It was in a green book, and the book was not in the yard.

"Pryce reads the why," said Kiva. "Before each one. It's her condition. She's on the wall."

"Then somebody fetch her," said Rhea. "Mace has Stroud."

It was three minutes. Anwen came through the gap with frost on one sleeve, and the book under her arm, and her face shut. She did not ask what had been said. She looked at the cord, and the half-covered hole, and the hand in the healer's two.

She wrote standing, against Perrin's back. It was two lines.

"*Wednesday the 5th. Three-door. For: to take an arm off the Ferren main, so that a gate can be shut and a bank filled. Likely cost: her left hand, to the elbow, and perhaps kept.*" She shut the book on her finger. "That's what's written. Is it still what's so?"

"Yes."

"Then I've nothing to add." Her level eyes came up. "Ask me."

"Pryce," said Kiva. Her throat had closed. "The wall. For whatever comes up after."

"Mine," said Anwen, and went.

"I'm frightened," said Kiva. "I'd like that written."

"It is," said Vane. "I'd begun the word before you said it."

"Then yes."

---

It took four minutes to set, and every piece of it was said aloud.

"Ten blades," said Vane. "One to a knot, from outside, dark. You light after the foot. You cut on one word, and the word's hers. Sorn has the twelfth, in my gate, since that's the one with the arm. Renn the eleventh. Talley the tenth. The rest as the Torch sets you."

"The word's *back,*" said Kiva. "Not before the gate's down, and not before my aunt says that the water's lying. On the slack."

"Ten cloths," said Pike. "Over the planks, where there are planks. Finch has the eleventh."

"The wire. Bexley can't work a key and a pen." Vane looked round. "Three words, to the pump house, as they're said. *Planted.* On that, the Works stops its pumps and quenches its bank; a dead cell can't be cooked. *Down,* when the gate's seated. *Back.* I want a runner at the lodge door who can shout."

"I can shout," said the boy called Ned.

He had got up from the shed wall. He had silt to the hair.

"I'm told that you think a bell's for other people," said Vane. "You'll do. Go."

The fitters had the bar. They stood two a side, in their shirtsleeves, with the ash pole lying fore and aft along the wall like a boat's tiller.

"On *planted,*" said the man in the apron. "Not a breath sooner. I'll not swing a bar through a girl."

Kiva walked to the hole.

The iron lid lay half across it. The arm went down through the foot of dark that was left, at the lane side of the post, humming. She stood on the lid. It rocked a little under her boots, and steadied. It had been under her cross for longer than anybody had kept the reason.

"The step first," said Orla.

"I know."

She had not chosen her ground. She knew which way the thing would throw her. It had its whole weight to the east, toward a gateway and ten grey shapes on a hill. A ward under it would be flung along that line, back toward the gap, as a cork goes down a stream. She had gone with the haul in the Pit, and it had served.

Four paces east of the post was packed dirt, under the wall. She stepped off the lid, and went to it, and laid two fingers of her right hand on her breastbone.

"Cold."

She opened the still door.

She set it by its corners, wide, as she had learned to over a cistern. It went down through the dirt.

There was a room underneath.

It was not near. It was eight feet down, and bricked, and full of something that moved. Her stance found the top of it and rang, faintly, as a foot rings on a cellar flap. And through the brick came the count, slow and even, and it wanted her to keep it.

She shut the door. She had been open for less than a breath.

"That's not clean," said Orla.

"No. It's the main. It comes in under the gate and along the wall to the post. I felt it on Sunday, on my knees. I'd have put the arm down on top of the drain." She was cold all over. "It'd have gone straight back in."

"Then there's no go."

"There's no go *there.*"

She went back to the post. She stood with her hand on the iron band, and made herself think it through like a sum, and not like a fright.

The main came in from the east. It went out to the west, along the south row, toward the sheds. And from the north-east, slantwise, a small mouth came in from the core house. That was three roads into one hole. There was a fourth side.

She walked four paces north from the lid, out into the middle of the lane, between the two rows. She opened the door again.

It went down through packed dirt, and clay under the dirt, and it found nothing at all. There was only ground. It took her as a wharf takes a mooring.

"That's clean," said Orla. Her palm was flat on the dirt beside Kiva's boot. "That's as dead as a doorstep."

"Then that's the mark." Kiva shut the door, and scraped a cross with her heel. Her heart was going very fast. "North. Across the lane. Not along it." She raised her voice. She pitched it for the gap, and the shed, and a square shape standing east of the post where the Pit had taught him to stand. "*The mark's turned! It isn't east! East's the drain! I'm carrying it north, to here! Brask! Wace! Say it back!*"

"North," said Lowen Brask. He was already walking. "Not along. To the cross."

"North," said Wace. "To the cross. Moss, with me."

"It'll throw you east," said Rhea Sorn. She had not gone to her knot. She stood at the low wall, with her blade arm down. "I watched the Pit. You went where it sent you."

"I bent the ward to send it. It's a spoon. If I curve it the other way, it'll turn the shove a quarter round." She did not know that. She had done it with a sandbag, from the left. "I think it will."

"*Think,*" said Rhea.

"I can't weigh it."

"No." The white-streaked head went down, a half inch, and came up. "Nor could I have. Go on."

There was a wall of frost to be thought of, and nobody had thought of it.

She saw it then. If the corner went north, the cord would run from a gateway to a cross in the lane, and the hem would hang from it. It would cut the Old Yard slantwise, from the low wall to her heel. The post would be on the far side.

"The bar's behind it," she said. "When I've planted. The hem comes across between the cross and the post. Whoever's at the bar is shut in against the wall, and can't come to me. Whoever has the corner mustn't be at the bar."

Brask had stopped.

"She's right," he said. "I'd have been turning a screw and looked up to find a wall between me and my own job." He looked at the four fitters. "Can you finish it? The two turns?"

"We've had four days to finish it," said the man in the apron. He did not sound proud. "I'd not wager a girl's arm."

"Then it's called," said Kiva. "Brask and Cade to the bar, now, south of where the hem will be. Wace and Moss to the cross. *Two to the bar.* I want it said back."

"Two to the bar," said Perrin. He had come up beside Brask. His big face was grey. "Cade and Brask. I keep the word from there. And I'd like it known—"

"It's known," said Kiva.

"I hadn't said what."

"I know what."

He nodded. He went and put his hands on the ash pole, and the fitters gave it to him as men give up a thing that has beaten them.

She went back to the lid.

---

"*Out,*" said Selka Dray.

Her voice was not strong. It was exact. It came across five paces of dark from a chair against a post, and it did not hurry.

"Two. Three. Four. *In.* Two. Three—"

Kiva put up her hands.

They came up low, at her right hip, over the foot of dark beside the iron. Two fingers stood clear of the rest, straight and stiff. She bent the ward into the bowl of a spoon, and she bent it the wrong way round, with the curve to the east and the open side north.

It was cold. She could feel the cold through the silver.

"Hand, to Ember, to still. Rowan, changing on three, and on five."

"*Out.* Two. Three. Four."

The arm drew up. The lid shivered under her boots.

"*In*—"

It was a hair early. It had been promised so.

"*One,*" said Kiva, and slid the pan under.

For four counts it lay there. It was the whole arm of a thing that stood ten abreast down a hill, and it lay in a tray between her palms, light as a clothesline, humming.

"*Two.*"

"*Out,*" said Dray.

They hauled.

It was not three posts. It was ten. The cord came up out of the dark like a hawser taking a ship, and found a spoon under it, and the whole of that haul went round the curve of her ward and into her right side.

It did not throw her east.

It turned. She felt it turn, a quarter round, as water turns in the bowl of a ladle, and it struck her in the ribs and the hip and pointed her out across the lane.

And it had a count in it. It was slow and even and many, and it was stronger than anything that she had ever had her hands on, and for the length of one tap it nearly had her.

"*Three.*"

She folded. Far edge. Near edge. A penny, in her palms. Shut.

Nobody.

She was nobody, going sideways over the packed dirt of the Old Yard with a hill's worth of haul in her spine. The ash went out of her like a breath. And the count went with it. It was left on the far side of a shut door, as mud is left on a step, exactly as it had been once before.

The struts caught her. Low, and long, and north. She was over the lane. Her left hand was cold to the wrist already, and she shut the Ember at the top and did not listen for it. She waited for the last thread to run home along her ribs and arrive.

"*Four.*"

Nobody, again.

It came out of a place that had been emptied in the Pit, and had only half filled. She had thought that she knew what that was like. There was a moment in the air when it seemed to her quite reasonable that there should be nothing to pay it with. Something paid. It went out through her left arm. She felt it pass the wrist, and go on, and she felt it pass the elbow.

*Arrive before you're there.*

She set the pan. Wide. By its corners.

"*Five!*"

Her right foot came down on a cross in the dirt, and the yard took her.

Then the corner came.

It came in at her heel like a tide coming up a slip. It was ten things leaning back on a line, and they went on leaning. It went down through her and into the clay and spread there, and the whole of the old ground under the wall took the weight of it, and held.

There was a white cord running down to the dirt a foot from her right boot. It was as taut as a stay. It ran away from her, east and a little south, over a low wall, to a black gateway, and from it a wall of frost was already dropping, thin as breath.

"*Planted!*" said Kiva. "*Six!*"

"*PLANTED!*" It was a boy's voice, from the lodge door, and it would have carried to the Sag. "*Planted, planted, send it!*"

"*Bar!*" That was Brask.

She did not look. She was counting.

She counted in fives. *Six. Seven. Eight. Nine. Ten.* The thing had fours. It bit on the first of each, as a girl in a chair had said that it would, and she went past it, a beat on, every time. It was like walking down a moving stair.

"Wace," she said. It came out between two numbers. "The plank. Your end."

*Boom.*

A still door opened on her left, a yard off, and the lane rang with it. It was a great deal wider than hers. Wace had been a post before.

*Boom.* Another, beyond him. Hendry Moss stood there with his thick neck sunk between his shoulders, and between the two of them something hummed, like a plank laid from trestle to trestle.

"Mine," said Wace.

She leaned it.

She had never done that. She had watched it done to her, on a floor that boomed, by a boy who pushed one man and moved another. She let the corner go over, a little at a time, from her own heel to the end of that plank, as one lets a full tin down onto a shelf.

It went. She felt it go off her. She felt two still-doors take it and sink a finger's breadth into the dirt.

"It's got a count," she said. "Don't keep it. Go in fives. I'll call them. *One. Two. Three. Four. Five.*"

"One," said Wace, through his teeth. "Two."

She shut the door.

She shut it on an empty doorway. She was standing on a cross in the Old Yard with nothing open, and a cord a foot from her boot that was not hers any longer.

She did not sit down. She had meant to. She had two people beside her now, and then three; a long incurious boy in black and gold had walked out of the dark without a word and set his feet at Moss's shoulder, and the plank had become a thing with three ends.

"Yarrow," he said. "Halden. I'm told it goes round."

"*One,*" said Kiva. "*Two. Three. Four. Five.*"

Five paces off, a girl in a chair was calling fours.

---

Perrin Cade had the end of an ash pole in both hands, and he had never wanted so much to say that he could not.

It was not heavy. That was the insult of it. He had held three hundred and eighty kilos over his head for thirty counts on Thursday, and a man had cut the figure in stone. This was a bar as thick as his wrist, stuck through a post, and it would not go round.

"It's not weight," said Brask. He was at the other end, with his feet set. "It's a screw. You can't lift a screw. You lean, and you wait for it."

"I've leaned."

"You've pushed. Open the door, Cade. Wide. And don't push at all."

Perrin opened it.

The dirt of the Old Yard hummed under him. It was a narrow place to stand. There was thirty feet of brick a yard behind his heels, and in front of him, three paces off, a wall of frost had come down across the lane like a sheet let fall from a line. He could see through it. There were shapes on the far side, and a white cord, and a small figure with one arm hanging.

*You don't look up.*

He looked at the bar.

"On my word," said Brask. "*Give.*"

He let his weight go into the pole. He did not shove. He stood there, wide, and let the screw have the whole of him, as he had let the Long Terrace have a plate.

Something groaned under the ground.

"*Take.*"

The bar moved a hand.

"*Give.* It's coming. Don't chase it. *Take.*"

They walked it. It was the slowest walk of his life. A quarter turn, with the pole coming round toward the wall, and his knuckles a foot from the brick. A half. The post creaked in its iron bands. Down the half-covered hole between them there was a sound like a ship's timbers.

His ribs were telling him something. They had been strapped from the armpits to the hips for a spring and a summer, and they had a good memory.

*That's not pain,* he thought. *That's only where pain was.*

"Eight," said the man in the apron, behind him. He was counting on his fingers like a child. "That's eight. One more, and it's in the groove."

"*Give.*"

It stopped.

It stopped as it had stopped for four fitters since Tuesday. The pole stood across the lane, and the two of them stood at its ends with everything they had in the ground, and nothing moved.

"There's a hand's breadth of silt in the west groove," said Perrin. His voice came out squeezed. "The boy said. He was on it when I had him out."

"Then it's a hand's breadth of silt against two still-doors," said Brask. "I'd back us. *Take.*"

It did not take.

Through the frost he could hear two counts going. There was a girl calling fours, thin and exact. There was another calling fives, and her voice was not steady, and she had two people answering her now, and then three.

She had planted it and given it away. He had heard her do that. She had stood on that lid and named every part that was somebody else's, and his was this.

"Brask," he said. "I'd like it known that I mind."

"What?"

"All of it. Everything since the fifth month. I've been saying that I don't, since Thursday, and it was a lie; I was only pleased." He shut his eyes. "I mind that I had my back to the Pit. I mind that I carried an old man past Pryce holding a string. I mind that there's a wall of ice between me and my own team and I'm pushing a stick."

"Then mind it into the bar," said Lowen Brask, "and stop telling me."

He did.

It was not strength. He had no more of that. It was only that he stopped keeping any of himself back for afterward. The still door went down through the dirt, and the clay, and the old brick of the wall's footing, and the whole of the bottom of the School came up under his boots.

The screw shrieked.

The bar went round. It went a quarter, all at once, and the two of them walked after it, stumbling, and under the ground an iron plate as broad as a door went down into its groove and struck bottom with a sound like a struck tank.

"*Nine!*" said the man in the apron. "That's nine! She's seated!"

"*DOWN!*" Perrin had a voice like a tram, and he had been saving it. "*Gate's down! Kiva! It's shut!*"

He let go of the pole. His hands would not open at first.

He found that he was sitting on the dirt with his back against thirty feet of brick. There was a line of something warm down his side, under the tunic, where two ribs had been.

"That's the strapping," he said, to nobody. "I'd forgotten that I'd left it off."

"Did it go?" said Brask.

"No. It's only telling me."

Through the frost, a girl was still calling fives.

---

Mara Rowan had never liked a room with twenty-four of anything in it. It was too many to keep in one's head and too few to stop trying.

She had them, all the same. She had got the last of her hands at six minutes to twelve, off a tram, in a body: five students in Wren House green who had stood a Bench in the ninth month and had been waiting at the Brickfields shed with the carts. There was an operator to every cell that she could find one for. There was a student at the rest, with a red handle under one hand and a fresh can at his feet.

Rusk had cell one. He had taken his coat off.

"I've not pulled a handle since I was made examiner," he said. "I find that I remember where it is."

The three pumps stood at the far end, turning. Old Lumb, the keeper, sat on an upturned bucket beside the sump hatch with a watch on his knee and a notched stick down the hole.

"Five-and-twenty minutes," he said. "From the stroke that they stop. I've told you that four times. It rained Sunday. At five-and-twenty the sump's at its sill, and then it's the Hythe's cellars, and I'll have a hundred people at my door in the morning with wet coal."

"I heard you the first time, Mr. Lumb."

"I'm only saying."

She had a chalk mark on the sump wall. It was a hand above the water. The water came up to it, and fell.

It had come up to it for nineteen days.

There was a sounder on the keeper's desk, a small brass arm on a spring. A Works clerk sat over it with his pencil. Beside him stood a narrow woman with a grader's seal on a cord and a brass glass like a clock with no hands; and behind her, with his hat in his hand, a pleasant man in a black coat who had asked very civilly whether he might watch.

The sounder chattered.

"*Planted,*" said the clerk.

Mara did not allow herself to think about what that word had cost, or whom. She had told a girl in a yard that she could only manage it from two miles off.

"Mr. Lumb. Stop your pumps."

He threw three levers, one after another. The slow breathing at the end of the room faltered, and ran down, and ceased.

It was very quiet. She had not heard that room quiet in her life.

"Handles!" said Mara. "Hands on. On my word, and not on your neighbor's. *Quench!*"

Twenty-four red handles came over in one flat sound, like a shutter.

All down the long wall the needles stopped nodding. They did not go one after another. They went as they had lived for nineteen days, together, and lay against their pins.

"Dead," said Rusk. "That's a bank that can't be cooked. Whatever comes down that drain now, there's nothing here for it to foul."

"There's the water," said Mara.

She went to the sump hatch. She knelt, in her good skirt, and held the lamp down.

It came up to the mark. It fell. It came up again, a little short.

"It's still moving."

"It's less," said Lumb. He had his stick in. "A finger. It was two."

"It's being let go of. Something's had hold of it, and it's been taken off, and the water's only rocking with what was in it." She did not take her eyes from the chalk. "I want it flat. I'll not fill over a rock."

"Three minutes," said Lumb. "Of five-and-twenty."

The sounder went again.

"*Down,*" said the clerk.

That was a gate. That was an iron plate in a groove at the head of a hill, and a core house on the dry side of it. She had drawn that in ink on Tuesday and had not believed in it until this breath.

She watched the water.

It rose toward the mark, and did not reach it. It sank. It rose less. It was like watching a cup that somebody had set down too hard. She counted a slow twenty, and at the end of the twenty the black surface lay in the lamplight as flat as a slate, with her own face in it.

"It's lying," she said. Her voice was not quite her own. "Send that. One word. *Still.*"

The clerk's hand rattled on the key.

"Five minutes," said Lumb.

It was a long way to send a word, and a long way back. She knelt at the hatch and watched her own face in a drain. She had no notion what was being done on a hill by a School. She had arranged it so. She had told herself that it was so that she could do her work.

The sounder spoke.

"*Back,*" said the clerk. And then, since it went on: "*Hem down. Ten cut. It is out. Fill. H.V.*"

"*Fill!*" said Mara.

She was on her feet. She did not remember getting up.

"Draw your dead cans! Fresh home! Every one's had its pinch; I salted them myself. Count your sixty aloud, each of you, and I don't care that it sounds like a schoolroom. Then tune. And *not* to the lamp tune. Every one of you has a card under your handle with a number on it. That's your count. There aren't two alike in this room."

They drew. They filled. The long room filled with voices counting sixty, all out of time with one another, and it was the most beautiful noise that she had ever heard in a pump house.

She went down the row.

She had known that she would have to. Twenty-four fresh cells stood side by side on one wall, and a cell was a patient thing, and it leaned to its neighbor. Somebody had to stand between each and the next, for a slow ten, while its tune took hold, and feel which way it was leaning, and tell the tuner.

She laid her left palm on the first drum.

"That's yours, Mr. Rusk. Three. It's holding three. Good."

The second. "Five. It's leaning down toward three. Bring it up. More. There."

It was like standing in a doorway between two rooms of people who each wanted her to come in. She held her hand on the iron and counted against both. She had watched a girl do that on the cinders of a pit, in fives, and had not understood until now what it had been taking out of her.

"Seven. Good. Four. Nine."

"That one's going back," said the narrow woman at the glass. She had not raised her voice. "Eleventh from the door. It's fallen in with the tenth. I have it on the dial."

"I felt it. Quench eleven. Fill it again." Mara did not stop. "There's a second can under your stool, boy. That's what it's for."

"Eleven minutes," said Lumb.

At the fourteenth cell she noticed that her fingertips were white.

At the eighteenth she could not feel the iron. She could feel that there was a drum. She had a girl at home who used those words.

*The count of a thing. Somebody else has that.* Rusk had told her that it was all over the Works. He had thought it rather funny.

"Mr. Rusk," she said. "You'll take the odd ones from here. Your thumbs are as good as my palm; I've said otherwise for eleven years out of pride. I'll take the even."

He came without a word.

"Lumb. The first pump. It's on cells one to eight, and no others. Start it."

One pump drew, and let go, in the quiet. It had a slow, private, limping sound. It was a three.

"Fourteen minutes," said the keeper. "That's one. I want three."

The second took its eight at nineteen minutes, on a seven. The third would not. The eleventh cell had fallen in a second time, and a third, and it was a white-faced Wren House student who said at last, in a very small voice, that he thought the can was cut.

It was. It was civic stock, and somebody had put soot in it.

"Twenty-five," said Lumb. "That's my sill. There's water going over it. I can hear it."

"I can hear it too. Give me a can off the spare shelf. Not that one. The one with the wax unbroken." She had her left hand on the drum. She could not feel it. She watched the needle instead, as she had taught a child to do. "Sixty. Tune it to eleven. Hold it. *Hold.*"

It held.

"Start your pump, Mr. Lumb."

The third pump drew. It was a five.

Three pumps took their strokes in the long brick room, each in its own time, like three people walking who do not know one another. It was six-and-twenty minutes by an old man's watch. Along the left wall four-and-twenty needles trembled, and stood, and not one of them nodded to the next.

"Seven cellars," said Lumb. He had his ear to the hatch. "Perhaps eight. A hand of water in each. I've known the Hythe have worse from a blocked gutter." He looked at her. "You're holding that arm oddly, missus."

She was. She had it against her chest, with the right hand over it.

It was white to the wrist. It did not hurt. It lay there like a thing that she was minding for somebody.

"That's starved," said the woman with the glass. She had come round the desk. She took the hand, quite briskly, and turned it to the lamp. "I've seen it on graders who hold a reed too long over a loud tin. It's not burned. It wants ward-trace, plain, inside the hour, or it'll sulk for a month." She frowned. "There's not a pinch of plain ward-trace in Meridian. It's all had the hair in it since the thaw."

"Then it will have to sulk," said Mara.

She was looking at the needles.

"I said to a watch officer that everything in my house had a meter and a way to shut it down," she said. "I've been nineteen days finding the second half." She was very tired. "I should like somebody to tell me what it cost."

Nobody in the pump house could.

---

"*Back!*" said Kiva.

She said it on Dray's *in.* She had waited for it. A boy had shouted *still* from the lodge door, and a watch officer had said, quite quietly, *Rowan, it's your word,* and she had let one whole slack go by to be certain of the next.

"*Give it to the ground!*"

Three still-doors let go together. The lane boomed.

There was light.

It came over the bottom wall. She could not see the street. She saw the brick, thirty feet of it, go white along its top, and the underside of the mist over Stopgate lit like a ceiling. One flash was blue-white and no longer than a blink. One was yellow, and level, and lasted most of a second, in a black gateway to the east. There were eight more.

Then the cord beside her boot lay down.

It did not snap. It went loose, from her cross to the gate and away down a hill that she could not see, and the frost that hung from it came after, slowly, folding as it fell. The Old Yard was whole again. There was a post on the far side of where a wall had been, and two people sitting against the brick.

And every window in the School showed a point of light.

She saw it. They had been brown, and dark, a hundred of them, on their boxes or on nothing. For the space of a blink each had a spark in it. Over the wall, down the whole length of a dark hill, six hundred more had done the same.

"*Dark!*" It was a square flat voice from the gateway. "*One!*"

And nearer, on the other side of the brick, a smaller one. It shook.

"*Dark! One! Corners!* Not you, the warden's corner, she's proud, bring her in a hand—"

That was Pru. That was a first-year who had run from a spaniel, calling a hem for a Fire's rung.

"There's a notch," said Noa.

He was at the shed. He had a white flag in one fist, as high as his arm would go, and he had not put it down to read.

"Two cliffs. Twenty-six, and twenty-six. Nothing between but the bound. And eleven seconds on from the second, there's a notch." His voice had gone to nothing. "It's the third time. It's eleven every time."

"That's the lamps," said Orla.

"No. That's what I can't get past. It isn't." He lowered the flag at last. "The notch was five minutes ago. It was while she was saying *Wace.* The lamps didn't so much as wink then. I was looking; I've done nothing but look." He turned his narrow face up to a hundred dark windows. "They jumped just now. At *back,* when the hem came down. At the Pit those fell in the same instant. I've had them on one page for six months, under one heading, as one thing."

"They're two," said Kiva.

"They're two. One of them keeps time with you. The other keeps time with that." He nodded at the wall, and the street beyond it. "I don't know what either is. But I know now that I've been adding a column to its neighbor."

"I counted you," said Selka Dray.

She had stopped calling. She sat in the infirmary's chair with her blanket round her and her leg stuck out, and her sunk eyes were on Kiva.

"You planted on my *in.* You handed it off at nine. You called fives for the best part of three hundred counts after that, with nothing open, for three boys who can't count." Her jaw worked. "You were late on the fourth five. I'd not mention it, only that you'd want it right."

"I would."

"I know. It's the worst thing about you."

Kiva found that she was sitting down. Somebody had her by the shoulders. It was Orla, on her knees in the dirt, with the stick thrown aside.

"Hand, Rowan."

She looked for it.

It was at the end of her arm. She lifted the arm from the shoulder, and it came. It was the color of a candle. It was that color to the wrist, and past the wrist, and past the elbow, to a place a hand's breadth above it where the sleeve began.

It did not feel like anything. It was only a thing that she could see.

She put her right hand to her collar.

"Cold," she said.

Then she put the same hand flat on the packed dirt of the Old Yard, beside the cross.

She had had something under that palm for ten days. It had come up through a doorpost, and an iron plate, and a flagstone, slow and even and many.

There was nothing. It was only ground.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/movement-006-pre-repair/chapter-47.md

# Chapter 47 — Kiva: Entered as Such

They would not let her stand for a quarter of an hour. When they did, Orla took her as far as the black gateway, with a blanket round her and the stick on the other side, and said that she might have one minute.

"You'll want to see what it was for," said Orla. "I should, in your place. One minute, and then you sit."

Stopgate lay below them under its lamps.

There was a pale stripe down the crown of the road, between the rails, where a wall of frost had lain down. Ten purse sheets stood along it in a row, drawn shut, each with a catcher kneeling at its neck and a red tag being written. A warden with a handbell was holding a lantern for Pru.

It was not finished. Kiva saw that at once. The blue was still there, in the middle of the pale, thin as a pencil line. With nothing to hold it, it had gone on giving for a little while, as a cut goes on bleeding after the knife is out.

"Three husks," said Orla. "They came up inside the first minute, one after another, and went to Sorn's gate like dogs to a dinner. Talley had one, and Gower, and a reserve blade of Twenty-Eight's whose name I never caught. Then that."

At the foot of the blue, seventy paces down, ten wards had a wall of silver across the street from curb to curb. A string was leaning on it.

It was a small one. It had four knots, and it stood on end, as a string ought, with its blunt head against the gate. Rhea Sorn stood behind its legs with her blade arm down. Beside her was a long-legged young man who had thrown away a stick.

There was a girl in the middle of the wall. Kiva could not see her face. She could hear her.

"Step," said Anwen. "Step. Hesketh, you're proud on the left. That's it. Step."

And below the gate, working up the hill toward it, six people and a handcart were bent over the road with their backs to everybody. Where they had been, there was no blue. There was only wet stone, drawn together behind them like a seam behind a needle.

"That's Bettan," said Kiva.

"That's the Line, doing what the Line is for. It's not yours and it's not mine. It's the one part of tonight that nobody had to invent." Orla turned her round, quite gently, by the good shoulder. "That's your minute."

They sat her on the dirt by the low wall, since she would not go farther. Healer Aske took her left arm across his knees. He went over it from the shoulder down, with his thumbs, for some minutes, and did not say a word.

The first slip came off the key at five-and-twenty past twelve, and Hollis Vane read it aloud. *Three pumps on three counts. Six-and-twenty minutes. The sump over its sill; seven cellars, perhaps eight. The bank is out. M. Rowan.* There was a noise in the yard at that, not quite a cheer. Vane took his hat off and put it on again.

The second came ten minutes after, and it was not in her aunt's words.

The boy called Ned brought the second himself. He did not know to whom it belonged. He held it out to the first person in the yard who looked like a Renn.

"It's signed like you," he said.

Kellan had come in from the street with his cuff unlaced and ash on one knee. He took the slip to the lamp. He read it, and Kiva saw his face change.

"That's my mother's hand," he said. "She's at the pump house with her glass." He read it out. "*Mrs. Rowan's left hand is starved to the wrist. She stood between four-and-twenty fresh cells and held each off its neighbor. It is not burned. It wants plain ward-trace inside the hour or it will not come right for a month, and I should not care to promise the month. There is none at the Works. Every can in the store has had the hair in it since the thaw. I am told that you will know where there is some. A. Renn, grader.*"

Nobody spoke.

It was her reading hand. It was the one that Mara had taught, after a cell on Linden Row, when the right would no longer tell her anything. There was a paper in the shop that said *Grade Two,* and a panel in the hall that had been relit in the ninth month, and both of them stood on that palm.

Kiva opened her mouth.

She shut it again. It was not hers to ask for. It was the most plainly not hers of anything that she had ever wanted.

"There's two drams," said Anwen.

She had come up from the street when the last of the four was cut, with the wall still in her shoulders. She was grey with tiredness, and there was frost melting on her sleeve.

"It's in the Low Lane. In the quench box, under my seal. I had it out of the press on Wednesday with the call drams, since a reserve is no use in a basement at midnight." She did not look at Kiva. She looked at Healer Aske. "It came off a husk on the Sag in the fifth month. Ward-trace. It's never had the hair. Is that what she wants?"

"It's exactly what she wants," said Aske. "It's the first time that I've been glad you kept it." He laid Kiva's arm back in her lap, gently, like something returned. "It's no use for this. I've looked. This has been turned out, and it'll come back by itself or it won't. That hand at the pump house is only hungry. I can do something for hungry."

"It wants four," said Perrin.

He was against the bottom wall, where he had been since the gate went down. Ruddock had his tunic up and was strapping him by lamplight.

"Four signatures. To break a seal. I'm one, if somebody'll bring me the book; I'm told that I'm not to get up." He drew as much breath as the linen allowed. "I'd like it known that I'd have said yes before she'd finished the sentence. I only waited so that it'd be asked properly."

"I'm two," said Kellan. He had not put the slip down.

"I'm not on the book," said Jude Talley. He had come in behind Kellan, heavy-footed, with his blade arm hanging. "I'm on the line by name. I said on Tuesday that I wondered what ash was for." He scratched his jaw with a grey thumb. "I've stopped wondering. If my name's wanted, it's there."

"Three," said Anwen. "That's mine."

They were all looking at Kiva.

It was a strange thing to be the fourth. She had thought, for one breath, that she ought not to sign at all. It was her aunt. It was her own household's hand being bought out of a team's tin.

*That's a shine,* she thought. *That's wanting to be seen not to take it.*

"I'm four," she said. "I'd have asked. I'm glad that I wasn't the one who did."

"I know," said Anwen. "I looked."

They did it on the lid of the Low Lane's quench box, as they had signed six hours before. Kellan held the lamp. Anwen broke the wax with her thumbnail, and read the entry as she wrote it.

"*Thursday the 6th, twenty minutes to one in the morning. Sealed medical reserve, W, 2.0. Opened by four. For: the reading hand of M. Rowan, operator, Grade Two, the Municipal Works, starved in taking the Ferren bank out of step. On the word of a grader and by the hand of Healer Aske. Medical.*" She looked up. "How much?"

"A dram and two-tenths," said Aske. "I'll not take more than I can use. The rest goes back under wax."

"*Drawn: 1.2. Re-sealed: 0.8.*"

Kiva signed last.

She had nothing to hold the page with. Her left arm hung from the shoulder like a coat on a peg. She stood looking at the book, with the pen in her right hand, and for a moment could not see how the thing was to be done.

A small round finger came down on the corner of the page.

"I've got it," said Pru. "Go on."

She wrote her name. It came out square and plain.

---

They heard it as they went down the hill to the van. It came up Stopgate behind them in a cracked voice that Kiva had last heard on the cinders of the Pit.

"*Closed! Ten to one, and it's shut!*"

She did not turn round. There was a tin to be carried.

They went along the Brickfields road in the Graywater van, with the rings whining and Ruddock at the tiller.

Aske had the tin on his knee. He had insisted on the arm coming too, and the girl attached to it. *I want that under a proper lamp,* he had said. *And you'll not sleep until you've seen her, so you may as well be of use and hold the door.*

The Ferren pump house had every window lit.

Kiva stopped on the sill, out of habit. Then she remembered that she had no certificate that mattered tonight, and that there was nothing in that room which could be harmed by a girl with no doors, and she went in.

It sounded wrong. That was the first thing. She had stood in that doorway ten days before and listened to three pumps breathe as one. Now there were three sounds. One was slow, and one was quick, and one limped, and they went in and out of one another like three people on a stair.

Along the left wall stood four-and-twenty needles under glass. Every one of them trembled a little, on a time of its own.

Mara was sitting at the keeper's desk.

She had her left hand in her lap, with the right laid over it. She looked up, and saw who had come in, and her eyes went at once to an arm in a blanket.

"So that's what it cost," she said.

"Aske thinks it'll come back. He doesn't know."

"To the elbow."

"A hand past it."

Mara did not say anything. She looked at it for as long as she might have looked at a drum.

"I said in this room an hour ago that I should like somebody to tell me," she said at last. "I find that I'd sooner have gone on not knowing until morning. That's not to my credit." She moved her right hand. "You'd better see mine. I'm told that we're to be a pair."

It was white to the wrist. It lay on her work skirt, pale and neat.

"I held them apart," said Mara. "Four-and-twenty. I gave the odd ones to Rusk at the eighteenth, when I couldn't feel the iron. I thought of you as I said it. I thought, that's what the child's been at since the Pit, and I've been telling her that she does it very nicely."

"You handed it off."

"I did. I hated it. He was perfectly competent, which was worse."

Aske had put the tin on the desk. He broke the second wax himself, and Kellan's mother held her brass over the open neck. The third reed sang, thin and clear. The seventh did not so much as stir.

"Plain," said the narrow woman. "I'd forgotten what that sounded like. It's like a room with the window shut."

It was not much to watch. Aske shook a pinch into a shallow glass. He took Mara's left hand in both of his, and held it over the glass, a little above, and Kiva saw his own hands go faintly silver, as a healer's do. He did not hurry. He was a man giving a hungry thing something that it could answer.

"There," he said, after a slow two hundred. "There's the first of it."

A flush had come at the root of the thumb. It crept, as damp comes up a wall.

"When," said Mara. Her voice was not quite even. "That's the heel. When. That's—I can feel your coat, man. I can feel the weave."

"I should hope so. It's a good coat." He did not let go. "A week before you read a drum with it. A month before you read four-and-twenty. You'll do as you're told, since you're licensed and know what a card is."

"I'm surrounded by people who say that."

He gave her the hand back.

She flexed it. She laid it, very carefully, palm down, on the keeper's desk, and shut her eyes, and Kiva saw her find the grain of the wood.

Then she opened them, and looked at her niece's arm, hanging.

"We've three hands between us that report," said Mara. "I make it three and a bit."

"I've got the bit."

"So you have." Something happened at the corner of her mouth. "Sit down before you fall. I've a thing to show you. I found it on Tuesday and I'd no room in my head for it."

It was a book. Old Lumb fetched it, from a press behind the pumps that held three centuries of them, bound in calf gone black. He carried it as a man carries a thing that he has been asked for at last.

"The keeper's book for 2604," he said. "The missus has had the lot out since she came. She says she's looking for how the bank's behaved. Nobody's asked me for a book in forty years."

"I went looking for a night when all four-and-twenty did something together," said Mara. "I thought there might have been one. There's a line in your own book, in the left column, that you've carried since the spring. *T-0.* Four of them. One's 2604."

She opened the calf at a straw.

It was a keeper's hand, round and careful, in ink gone brown. Kiva read it by the desk lamp.

*The 11th of the third month. Keeper took ill in the evening; a fever, three nights. Entered here because the needles did it too.*

*Dreamt that the roof was off the house and there was nothing over it. Not dark. Nothing. And all four-and-twenty stood up together, and were looking. I do not know at what. I woke and they were as usual.*

*The levee lamps went out that night and came on again of themselves. No fault found. Not grid. I have put that in the fault column and this in remarks, as I am told that a fever is not a fault.*

Kiva read it twice.

She was aware of her own breathing. There were three pumps going in the room, and none of them kept time with it.

"*The roof was off,*" she said.

"You know it."

"I read a page in the Library, in the sixth month. I'm not to copy it. I'm allowed to have read it." She kept her eyes on the brown ink. "There's a phrase. *The sky unroofed.* That was a Master of an Order, in a house on a hill, a hundred and sixty years before this. She'd no notes. She said that it was paying attention." Her mouth was dry. "*Were looking.*"

"And you."

"I'm not on a page that you can open. You sealed yours."

"So I did. I'm rather glad of it tonight." Mara turned the book a little, so that the lamp fell square. "Then here's what I have, and it isn't much. A woman who could hold a ward over a flood wall for nine days. A pump keeper with a temperature. They're a century and a half apart. Neither had heard of the other; he'd no more have seen an Order's papers than he'd have dined with the Chair. And they've both written down that there was no roof."

"And every lamp."

"And every lamp. On a night that the Works has in its fault column." She shut the calf, gently. "It doesn't tell me what it is. It doesn't tell me that it's coming, or when, or whether it's a thing that happens or only a thing that people dream when a fever's on them and the needles jump. I'd not build a shed on it."

"What does it tell you?"

"That it isn't you." Mara looked at her. "I've thought since you were thirteen that you'd been singled out for something, and I've hated whatever did the singling. It's older than you. It's wider. It was in a pump house in 2604, with a man who kept a tidy book." She laid her newly eased hand on the cover. "I find that a great comfort, and I can't altogether say why."

"There'll be others," said Lumb.

They looked at him. He was not a man who had expected to be looked at.

"Keepers' books. There's one in every pump house on the coast, and they all keep a remarks column, and nobody reads it. If there was a night in 2604, somebody at Saltmere had a temperature as well." He rubbed his nose. "I'm only saying."

"That's the second sensible thing that you've said tonight, Mr. Lumb," said Mara. "The first was five-and-twenty minutes."

There was a man standing by the wall of needles. He had been there the whole time, with his hat in his hand, and he had taken care to be in nobody's road.

"I should like to add one line," said Mr. Adlam. "Under my own name, as before. I was watching that bank when the word *back* came through." He nodded at the row. "It had been quenched for five minutes. There was nothing in a single can. And four-and-twenty needles lifted off their pins, together, by a hair, and lay down again."

"T-0," said Kiva.

"I've learned what you call it. I've no better name." He turned his hat. "Mr. Bexley tells me, by a note that came down with the healer, that his pen moved five minutes earlier. I had always taken those for one event. So had he. It appears that we have each been second to a mistake." He smiled, quite pleasantly. "I find that I prefer it to being first to a certainty. I shall write to him in the morning."

---

Hollis Vane read his slate at five on Thursday, standing in the black gateway, since there were people on that hill whose street it had been.

They had come back. Sixty-two households stood on the wet setts of Stopgate in the last of the light, with their coats on, and looked at a road that had a pale thin stripe down the crown of it, seventy paces long, like the mark that a tub leaves on a floor. The planks were still down. The oak of the gate had split with frost on either hand.

Kiva stood by the low wall. Aske had put the arm in a sling, more to keep it from being knocked than for any good that it would do.

"*Stopgate, the night of the 5th,*" said Vane. He had his outdoor voice. "*The bell at two minutes past eleven. A tear lying in the road, held by the main under it. A string of twelve, abreast, every knot reading. Closed, ten minutes to one, by the Home Line.*"

He turned the slate to the light.

"*Lives: none lost. Carried: sixty-two houses, fourteen by litter, and a school. Hurt: K. Rowan, catcher, the left arm; see the direction. M. Rowan, operator, the reading hand, starved; eased inside the hour. Cade, carry, two ribs sprung and not broken. Works: the School's core put down by its keeper and raised again at noon. The Stopgate sluice shut, the first time in the Works' memory, and opened at first light. The Ferren bank quenched and filled on four-and-twenty counts; it is out. The pumps stood six-and-twenty minutes. Eight cellars in the Hythe, a hand of water.*"

"Eight," said somebody on the hill.

"Eight. I've been to each. There's a coal merchant who has a good deal to say." Vane did not smile. "*Drum twenty-nine, fouled. A plank split with the frost under the first knot, and the best part of thirty drams went down. Its four streets are on boxes; the reserve says a month. Seven drums below it are in step still, each alone on an open link. The Works will take them out one in a day.*"

He turned the slate over.

"*Tinned, clean: a hundred and ninety-two. That's off what came up after the hem was down; three husks and a string of four that stood on end as a string ought. It's been combed. It reads as this year's ash reads, and no louder. Put by, red, on catchers' words and a comb: five hundred and sixty and four-tenths. And a stripe of something that isn't in the yield book, which you're standing beside.*"

"And the remark?" said a warden.

"One." He read it flatly. "*The one hole on the hill that could not be planked was left open, with a boy in it, to clear a gate. The thing went to it. The reason for leaving it was good, and I'd give the same order tomorrow. The remark is the event's.*" He hung the slate on his belt. "I'd sooner say that to your faces than have you read it on a table."

Then he did what he had done on the cinders of the Pit.

"The pot. It was a response, and it's shared the Line's way. Ground first. The civic half's ninety-six, and it goes into this hill's boxes and the Hythe's, tonight, before anybody's else. Casualty reserve, four-and-twenty. Schools, six-and-thirty. Teams, six-and-thirty." He looked along the fours. "Four of you stood it under your officers. That's nine drams to a team. The Halden four cut two of those knots and caught one, and draws the same as Twenty-Eight, whose blade called a count from a chair."

"The Halden Works does not object," said Mr. Adlam.

"I'd have been astonished," said Foreman Birch, beside him. "And he tells me that he's given up being astonished."

The red row stood along the foot of the wall. There were twelve bags in it. Each had a tag, in a catcher's hand, and Kiva could read the nearest from where she stood. It was round, and careful, and it had been written slowly.

*Red. Stepped. On a catcher's word and a comb. P. Finch.*

"She cried when she'd done it," said Pike.

He had come to stand at the low wall. He had a roll of cord under his arm.

"Not at the bag. The bag was forty-eight, off a Fire's cut, over a planked grating in the dark, with a warden for a corner. I've had worse off my own line." He looked at the tag. "It was the pencil. She said that she'd never put her name on anything that might stop a thing. I said, well, now you have, and you'll find that it stays put."

"She told Kellan *not yet.*"

"Twice. I heard her. He came down like a lamb." Pike shifted the cord. "Sorn's writing to the board tonight. A team's attached until it's stood a whole Flask under a foreman without a remark against it. You've stood a Flask and a half. The remark's the event's. I'd not be surprised if somebody took a pair of brackets off a line."

He went away along the wall before she could answer.

There was a woman at the back of the crowd with flour to the elbows. She had come up from Coper Street on the tram, and she stood beside a girl in a brown coat, and Kiva saw her say something and saw Anwen answer.

It was not a long answer. Mrs. Pryce put both her floury hands over her mouth.

Anwen looked across the gateway then. She did not smile. She lifted her chin a quarter of an inch, as one hand on a line acknowledges another.

*I've told her,* that meant. *It's hers now, and I'm done carrying it about.*

---

The elbow came back on Friday.

It came toward evening, like a tide making up a slip, as the wrist had once. Kiva was at the kitchen table with her book. She felt the edge of the wood against the point of her arm, and sat without moving for some minutes, in case it should go again.

The wrist came on Saturday night. The palm came on Sunday morning, and the thumb with it, and the last two fingers. By the time that she had her boots on she could feel the lace in them.

She could see the first two. She laced round them.

Aske took the sling off on Monday, at seven. He had the towel, and the pin. He had a penny, and a cup of water from the kettle, and a scrap of waxed cord.

"Eyes shut."

She felt the pin on her little finger, and the next. She felt it on her thumb. She felt it, sharp and at once, in the palm and at the heel of the hand, and on the wrist, and at the elbow.

Then there was a long time when nothing happened.

"Something's pushing," she said.

"And here?"

"The same. Less."

He put the penny between her two fingers and her thumb.

"Edge or flat?"

"I don't know."

"The cup."

"I can't tell."

"The cord. Is it crossed?"

She could not have told him that there was a cord. She opened her eyes. It lay over, and then under, as plain as print.

Aske dried her fingers on the towel, one at a time.

"Then I'll tell you, and it's the same thing that I told you a fortnight ago, and I've never in my life been so pleased to repeat myself." He capped his pen. "They bend. They grip. They tell you when something's pushing. They'll not tell you edge from flat, or hot from cold, or one thread from two. And the rest of it's come back to where it was, and not a hair less. I looked for the hair."

"As before."

"As before. I've a case now. One." He wrote. It was a longer line than usual. "It took the arm for four days and gave it back. I don't know why it gave it back. I don't know that it would a fourth time; I think it took a deal more than it took in the Pit, and returned it slower, and I can add." He looked at her over the card. "Nothing lit until the new year. Not a step. Not a ward for a tap. You'll catch with your hands dark and your voice, and you'll let that arm find out what it is."

"That's three months."

"It's eleven weeks and four days. I've counted them so that you needn't." He gave her the card. "I'm told there's a first-year who does up your cuff."

"Stroud did it once. Anwen does. Pru, if I'm slow."

"And you let them?"

"I'm getting less bad at it."

"Mm," said Aske. He turned away to his linen. "They always say that on a Monday."

She went up the stair that creaked that afternoon, since it was within the week.

It was Vane who witnessed her. He had the canvas book open, and beside it the ledger that was older than the School, turned to the back.

She wrote in the fourth column first. *Cold. Wednesday the 5th, in the Old Yard, about midnight. After three doors in sequence, under direction, with an arm of a string taken off a drain, and carried, and planted, and given to three others. Cold before, during and after.*

She held the pen a moment.

*I did not ask it anything. It did not do anything. K.R.*

"You've a habit," said Vane.

"It's what's so."

"I know that it is. I've come to rely on it." He turned the older book toward himself. "I've the third column to fill. I wrote the cost before. I'd like to write it after, in your hearing."

He wrote it standing, as he had at a shop counter.

*Cost, after: the left arm, without feeling to a hand above the elbow. Four days. Returned to where it was. The first two fingers as before.*

He blotted it. He did not shut the ledger.

"There's a line under the first of these," he said. "E.K. wrote it, in the autumn of 2471, about a woman with two doors. *Entered on the Line's roll as catcher. Hand as before.* I told you on a Sunday that I'd no roll to add you to. I'd not thought about it. I'd only said it."

"I'd a book of my own."

"So you had, and you wrote your own line in it, and I thought, well, that's her answer." He rubbed his jaw. "The watch sat yesterday. I moved a thing. I'll not tell you how the hands lay; you'll hear it from the Ferren, since she wants to say it herself, and at seventy she's entitled." He closed the ledger at last. "Thursday, at four. The hall. Bring your sponsor. And I'd wear a collar that shows the cord."

---

Noa had three columns by Saturday, and he brought them to the Rowan kitchen table as another boy might have brought a kitten.

"I'm not saying it's a reason," he said. He said it before he had sat down. "I want that understood. I've been wrong since the sixth month about two things that I'd added together, and I'd as soon not do it again with three."

He unrolled a sheet. It was ruled in his small square hand.

The first column was people. *Median Hold, the class of 2680: 6.1, against forty years at 5.3 to 5.5. Fire readings at seventeen: three. Stonehand's plate: 340 for thirty-one years; 350, 355, 362, 380, since the thaw.* There were eleven lines of it.

The second was the other thing. *Tears on the district board since the thaw: fourteen, against a usual four or five. Skins: none in sixty years; nine this year. A Flask that walked a bus. A hold stood by husks. A string abreast.* There were eleven lines of that as well.

"They both go up," said Noa. "They went up slowly until the fifth month. Since the fifth month they've gone up faster. I've drawn them on one sheet, with one rule along the bottom for the weeks, and I've been very careful not to draw anything between."

"And the third?" said Mara.

She had her left hand flat on the table, under the lamp. She was reading the wood with it. She had been doing that for two days, whenever she thought that nobody was looking.

"That's what I hadn't got. Adlam said that if I'd a column from somewhere that wasn't Meridian, somebody would have to read it." He drew a second sheet from inside his coat. It was on green-edged paper, in a hand that Kiva did not know. "There was a Saltmere officer on the hill on Wednesday. She'd brought their house four up for the Regional and stayed for the tables. Yeo. She cut the fourth knot."

"I saw a green coat."

"She came to find me on Friday. She'd heard that there was a boy with a page." He laid it down. "She'd wired her own hall. That's what came back."

It was a short column. *The harbour bank at Saltmere: thirty-six cells. In step from twenty past two on the 17th of the ninth month. Not come out. The harbour pumps have not dropped a stroke.* And below it: *Tears on the coast board since the thaw: nineteen, against seven. Skins: eleven. The Saltmere school's load plate: beaten four times.* And at the foot, smaller, as a thing is written that the writer will not vouch for: *A ship's engineer off a hull from over the water says that his own port's bank did the same, and puts it at the same hour, reckoned back. Not entered as fact.*

"Twenty past two," said Mara. "On the seventeenth."

"Two hundred miles off. In the same minute that four husks stood a square in the Pit." Noa's narrow face was grey with not sleeping. "I don't say that the Pit did it. I'd be a fool. I say that it wasn't only the Pit. Whatever fell on the seventeenth fell on two towns at once, and I'd want to know how many more before I wrote a word about why." His finger rested on the last, small line. "If that engineer's telling the truth, it isn't a matter of towns. I haven't a sheet wide enough for what it's a matter of."

"Who's had this?"

"The Chair. She's put it with the others, and she says that for the first time she knows what to do with them." He almost smiled. "She's sending all three sheets to the Charter Board, under her own seal, with Adlam's name second and a line of Yeo's. She says that a Circuit can't ask a nation to look at its banks. A Chair can ask who else has a keeper with a book."

"Lumb said that," said Kiva.

"Then I'd like his name on it as well. It's the only notion in the whole sheet that anybody could do on a Monday." Noa rolled the paper. "Yeo's gone south this morning, on the boat. She says that they've nobody at Saltmere who knows how to take a bank out. She asked whether the Works would lend its reader."

"Its reader has one hand, on a card, until the eleventh month," said Mara. "I told her so. I said that I'd write it down."

She had. Kiva had seen it on the dresser, eleven sheets, in the small exact print. *How the Ferren bank was taken out of step, with what was got wrong first.* It had seven columns. The last was the widest.

"She says that she'll ask again in the spring," said Noa. "She says that there's a Measure down there in the third month, and that half of Meridian seems likely to be at it."

He put the roll under his arm, and stood.

"I've a heading," he said. "For the page. I've had *Not yet weighed* since the Pit, and it's still the truest thing on it. But I've put a second line under."

"What?"

"*Not one thing.*" He went a little red. "It isn't much. It's only that I had to cross out a six-month mistake to write it, and I left the mistake in, with a line through, so that whoever reads it next can see where I'd been."

He went down the shop stair.

Kiva looked at her own book. It lay on the table by the brass weight, soft at the corners. It had a spring and a summer in it, and now an autumn.

She had never torn a page out of it. She had not thought, until that moment, that it was a thing worth noticing.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/movement-006-pre-repair/chapter-48.md

# Chapter 48 — Kiva: Why It Is Crooked

There were more than seven in the room on Thursday, and that had not happened in the Ferren's time.

Kiva knew the oak table, and the window over the levee, and the black box. She had not seen the bench along the wall with anybody on it. Orla sat there now, with her stick. Beside her was a small upright woman with two pairs of spectacles, and beside the Reader, very straight, with his cap on his knee and a roll of tape that he had not been asked to bring, was Noa.

The School's bursar had the end of the bench. He had a flat deal box on his lap, tied with tape, and he held it as a man holds a thing that he would sooner not open.

"We go by our watches in here," said the Ferren. "That's for the seven. The rest of you are whoever you are, and I'll thank you to be it briefly." She had her hands folded on the table. "Two things. The first is ours. River Flats."

The broad, careful man had a paper in his cuff, as he had once before. Kiva had been told about that paper. He laid it on the oak.

"I moved a recall in the seventh month," he said. "I'd have the bearer know that I was the one, and that I thought myself right. I said that the plate had never been carried by anybody with so many people looking at her. I said that it should lie in its box until the season was read." He did not look at her. He looked at the paper. "The season's read. I've a second motion. I'd sooner make it myself than have it made at me."

"Go on."

"The watch doesn't choose a bearer. It's on the first leaf, and I'd not alter it. But the Line keeps a roll, and the watch may say who's on it." He unfolded the sheet. "*That K. Rowan be entered on the Home Line's roll, as catcher. Not a seat. Not a wage, save a line-hand's day rate when she's called. To be called by a Line foreman, with her sponsor's leave while she has a sponsor, for the work that she's shown: to set a mark, to read ground, to call a count that is hers and name the ones that are not.*" He cleared his throat. "*For cause written. The cause is two entries in the Directions to the Unplaced, and what's in the third column of each.*"

"The Sag will read the cause," said the Ferren.

Vane read it. He read both directions, in his outdoor voice, with the cost before and the cost after, and he did not lean on any word.

Seven hands went down flat on the oak.

The Lantern's went first. She looked rather pleased to have a thing that she could be quick about.

"Entered," said the Ferren. "Sag. Write it where E.K. wrote the other. There's room; nobody's used the page in two hundred years."

He wrote. He turned the old ledger so that Kiva could see the line come.

*Rowan, K. Entered on the Line's roll as catcher, autumn 2680. Hand as before.*

She had written those last three words herself, on a Sunday night in the ninth month, in a canvas book, since nobody else had a place to put them. It was a different thing to watch them go down in a watch officer's hand, under a line of 2471.

She found that her eyes had stung. She kept them on the ink until they had stopped.

"It isn't a rank," said the Ferren. "You'll not get a better seat at anything by it. It's a list of people whom we send for. I've been on it since I was nineteen. I've never been thanked for it, and nor will you be." The river pilot's eyes went to the bench. "Now the second thing, which is not ours, and which a gentleman has been sitting on like a hen."

The bursar got up.

He was a thin worried man, and he had been a good deal thinner and more worried since a Saturday night in a yard.

"I asked this watch in the seventh month whether I might borrow its plate for the School's anniversary case," he said. "I was answered out of our own fourth edition. I didn't care for the answer. I'd the ninth, which says that the plate of the first teachers is preserved by the School." He put the deal box on the table. "It's been in the conservation press for longer than I've had the keys. The Reader has asked to see it every spring for thirty years. I've told her every spring that it was being conserved. So had the bursar before me."

"Thirty-one," said the Reader.

"Thirty-one. I beg your pardon." He untied the tape. "I was shown a post last week. It had been retained for ceremonial continuity since 2603, and it turned out to be the handle of a gate. I thought that I had better go and look at what else we retain."

He lifted the lid.

There was a bed of yellowed wool. In it lay a disk the size of a penny, of a dull white, with nine rings cut into its face.

It was plaster.

Kiva did not need to be told. One could see the grain of it, and a chip at the edge where it had been dropped. It was a cast.

"There's a label," said the bursar. His voice was not steady. "On the underside of the lid. I've read it eleven times."

He read it a twelfth.

"*Cast of the plate of nine rings that was carried by the first teachers. Taken by leave of the bearer, for the School's case, that students may know its look. The plate went out again with the Home Line the same day, on its cord. It is not the School's and never was. We do not show the plate. We show this.*" He put the lid down. "Somebody stopped reading that label, and somebody else wrote a ninth edition. I don't think that either of them meant a lie. I think that they tidied."

Nobody at the table spoke. It was a word that had been used a good deal lately.

"I've a request," said the Reader.

She had risen. She was not much taller standing.

"I can't lay a hand on the Line's plate. I've read the watch's ruling of the first of the seventh month; I had it copied. No hand but the bearer's. I ask whether the bearer may lay it beside that cast, in her own hand, for as long as it takes an old woman to count to nine."

"That wants a vote," said the Ferren. "To show it."

Seven hands were flat already. They had not been lifted.

"Then that's voted. Miss Rowan. If you're willing. It's yours to refuse; nobody in here chose you."

Kiva drew the cord out from her collar.

She had worn it for four years. She had shown it to a steward once, with a paper, and to a watch officer, and had never laid it on a table in a lit room for anybody to look at. It came over her head, and she held it in her right palm, and set the back of that hand down on the oak beside the box.

It lay there, dark, with its nine grooves.

The Reader bent over it. She had both pairs of spectacles on. She did not touch.

"Nine," she said. "And nine. The fifth ring's wider than the rest; so is the cast's. There's a flaw across the third, at about four o'clock, like a hair. Look at the plaster, child. No. You look. I've seen what I came for."

Kiva looked. There was a raised thread across the third ring of the cast, where the plaster had gone into a scratch.

There was a scratch on the disk. She had known it since she was thirteen. She had thought that she had made it herself, on a stair.

"It's the same," said Noa.

He had come off the bench without being asked, and stood a pace behind the Reader, with his hands shut behind his back so that nobody should think he meant to reach.

"I drew that. In the sixth month. I drew the nine rings off the plate in the fourth edition of the Founders' History, with the fifth one wider, and I wrote beside it, *Is hers the same?*" His narrow face had gone red. "I never asked to see. It wasn't mine to ask. Then the watch said that it wasn't anybody's. I've had it on a page ever since, with a question mark."

"You may take the question mark off," said the Reader. She straightened, a hand in the small of her back. "I'll say it, since I'm the only one in this room whose trade it is to say that one old thing is another. That is the plate of nine rings that the first teachers carried. It was never preserved by anybody. It was *released.* It has gone from hand to hand by appointment, with a watch to witness and not to choose, for as long as there has been a Line, and it has come down that road to a catcher of seventeen who was named by a woman who liked a question that she'd asked." She took off the upper spectacles. "I have asked the School for it for thirty-one years. I have been looking at a girl's collar since the sixth month."

"The tenth edition will say so," said the bursar. "Out of the fourth. Word for word. I've the printer on Monday."

"*It is released to one who carries it,*" said Orla, from the bench. Her voice was rough. "*We have not been told why and have not thought it ours to ask.* I learned that off a first-year who'd been to a library. I was twenty years on the Line and nobody'd told me."

"What's it for?" said Kiva.

She had not meant to ask. She had asked it of a levee catcher's line, and a watch officer, and an empty kitchen. It was out before she had thought that this was a room where somebody might know.

The Reader looked at her quite kindly.

"I can tell you whose it's been. I can tell you that it's the one in the picture. That's the whole of what I've given you, and you had most of it already." She folded the spectacles. "The first book's lost. I've looked for that as well."

"I asked a thing in the summer," said the Ferren. "I'd had four-and-thirty years of *what does it do.* I said that I'd begun to wonder what else was happening when it did." She was looking at the dark disk on the girl's palm. "It's had two bearers put *warm* in my book, and each put it once, after a first crossing, and each put *cold* for every crossing since. That's all that I know. I'm seventy-one in the spring. I've decided that I can bear to leave a question in a book with nothing under it."

Kiva closed her hand.

It was cold. It had been cold on a table in front of twelve people, as it had been cold in the Old Yard at midnight with an arm of a string under her heel. She put the cord over her head.

She had been told what it was. It did not feel any different. She had rather thought that it would not.

---

The board in the Measure Hall basement had a crowd round it on Friday, and for once nobody was reading over anybody's shoulder to see whether a name was there.

*The Meridian Collegiate Circuit, 2680. The season is read.*

*The Regional Measure: 1. Halden License. 2. Old Four. 3. Team Twenty-Eight. 4. T7.*

"They've gone," said Perrin.

He was standing very upright, since he was strapped again, from the armpits to the hips.

"The brackets. Off Old Four, and off us. *Second step, in its own right.*" He read it with his lips. "I said in the ninth month that I didn't mind them. I said that it'd be like losing a tooth." He considered. "It is. I keep feeling for it."
