"There's a line under," said Kellan.

There were three.

The first was a clerk's. *The first company goes forward to the National Measure, at Saltmere, in the third month, 2681. Entries under each team's own hand.*

The second was level and upright, and Kiva knew it from the foot of a notice in the spring.

*Entered by the Chair. Rowan, K., Third Division, the Crooked Line: seated. No longer provisional. Catcher. Her sponsor's line and every condition on her certificate stand as written. She is not thereby classified. I still have no column. I have a page, and it has a second line on it. Y. Crest.*

Nobody moved their kit away. Nobody had, for some weeks. She noticed that she had stopped looking to see.

"*Seated,*" said Anwen. "On what?"

"On having said who had the corner," said a voice behind them.

It was a hard, exact voice. It came from about the height of Kiva's elbow.

Selka Dray was in the infirmary's chair. Wilf Marten had the handles. She had her leg out before her in its board, and a rug, and a face like a shut door.

"I've read her page. The Chair let me, since I asked, and since my name's on a rule. It doesn't say what you did with three doors. It says that you stood on a lid and told a watch officer every piece of the thing that wasn't yours, and who had it, before you'd put a hand up." Her jaw moved. "I'd have seated you on that. I'd not have seated you on the other."

"It's the same night."

"It isn't the same thing. I've been told that you're particular about that."

"The third line's hers," said Marten. He said it to the board, apologetically, as he said most things.

It was in pencil, fair-copied by a clerk. *On the request of S. Dray, blade, Team Twenty-Eight: that at the National Measure any candidate with more than one door be read from a sanctioned mast, by a reader of this office, as at the Regional. Granted, and sent to Saltmere under seal. She states that she has objected to the candidate twice and has not changed her mind. She states that she called a count for her on the 5th because she was the one who could. Both are entered.*

"I said that I'd write it," said Kiva. "You said that you'd take my word."

"I did. Then I thought that a thing like that ought to be in a hand that isn't yours." Dray looked up at her. The grey eyes were as sunk as ever, and quite steady. "Twenty-Eight goes to Saltmere. So does Seven. I shall be on my feet by the third month. I mean to beat you in a lane there, on a declared count, with a needle on you, in front of whoever they've got instead of eleven hundred people."

"I'd expect nothing else."

"That's a Halden man's phrase."

"I know. I've been keeping it."

Something happened to Selka Dray's mouth. It was not a smile. It was the place where one would have been, on somebody else.

"Push," she said, to Marten. "I've a healer at eleven. He says that I'm to bend it, and I intend to argue."

---

Rhea Sorn sent a boy with a card that evening. It was four words long. *Yard. Six. Bring Finch.*

There was no van out. There was only the hall yard, and the hooded lamp, and a tall woman sitting on an upturned tub with her elbows on her knees. Pike stood behind her with his hands at his back.

"I told you on the cinders that I wasn't offering you anything," said Rhea. "I wasn't. You were seventeen, with a hand in a cloth, and your team had my name on its entry." She did not get up. "You're seventeen still. The brackets came off this morning. So I've nothing on you any longer but what I choose to put, and I've thought for a week about how little that ought to be."

She held out a card. It was slate blue, and it had a good deal more than twelve words on it.

Kiva took it in her right hand, and read it under the lamp.

*Rowan, K. Catcher's second, to Pike. The night van, two nights in the month, from the first of the new year, when her healer's card is up. Dark hands. No door on my floor that is not on her certificate. Finch with her, for the cord, if Finch will. If T7 is called on a night of mine, she is T7's and I go short. Until the last of the third month. Then we look again, and either of us may say no. No seat is promised. R.S.*

It was a small thing. It was an edge of a wall and a lamp, written out long.

"That's all of it," said Rhea. "I've put the ends on it on purpose. I burned a card once with two words on it about you. One of the two was *mine.* I'd had no business writing it, and I knew it the same night." The pale eyes came up. "This one's got *either of us* on it. I'd like you to notice that I can learn."

"What's a catcher's second?"

"She kneels at my other hem," said Pike. "She sets nothing. She calls nothing. She watches a man who's been thought a great hand for six-and-twenty years, and she tells him afterward what he did that he didn't say aloud." He nearly smiled. "I'm told that there's a deal of it. I've a first-year to thank for the notion."

"And Pru?"

"Ask her. I did, an hour ago, at her mother's door."

Pru was standing a pace behind Kiva's shoulder with her hands in her sleeves.

"I said that I'd have to think," she said. "Then I said yes so quickly that I bit my tongue. Then I said that I'd only come if I was asked by you as well, since I'm not your cord. I'm a catcher with a sheet. I've got a red tag in a cold room." She went scarlet. "That came out bigger than I meant."

"It came out the right size," said Kiva.

She looked at the card again.

She had wanted a thing like this since she sat on the Measure Hall steps in the spring and watched a boy with steady eyes shake hands under three striped awnings. She had wanted it on ordinary terms. She had thought that when it came she would know what to say.

*What's behind it?* That was Anwen's question. She found that she had it ready, and that it was a better thing to have ready than an answer.

"Two things," she said. "Before I say. I'd like the *before* column read out, each night, on the slate, as you read it on the fifth. Whatever's in it. To all of us, not to me."

"That's on the slate already. It's ruled. I'd look a fool rubbing it out."

"And I can't say yes for the four. If Seven wants me those nights for a thing that isn't a call, a floor, or a lane before Saltmere, they've the first word. I'd have to ask them. I don't know what they'll say."

Rhea looked at her for some time.

"I had a speech for this," she said. "In case you said yes at once. It was about not being grateful." She got up off the tub, stiffly. "I shan't want it. Take the card home. Ask your four. Bring it back signed or bring it back torn, and tell me which on the first of the month." She put her hands in her pockets. "I'll say one thing, since I'm told that I'm allowed a piece of advice a season. You'll hate being a second. You'll kneel at a hem and see every single thing that you'd do differently, and you're not to open your mouth until the bag's sealed."

"I've been a corner. For Pru."

"I heard. I heard that you were corrected eleven times."

"Fourteen, by the end."

"Good," said Rhea Sorn. "That's about the right number."

---

Four of them signed the book on Saturday, at the Rowan kitchen table, and Kellan read the whole of it, since it was the last of the season.

"*Saturday the 15th of the tenth month. Call reserve, 2.5: spent, 0.70. Pryce, forty-one hundredths, on a wall. Cade, fourteen, on a bar. Talley, nine. Renn, six. Rowan, none. Returned to held, 1.80.*" He turned the page. "*Three-door line, 0.59: spent, whole, on the 5th, under direction. Lent to the casualty reserve, 4.0: repaid on the 4th. Credited from Stopgate, by the foreman's pot: 9.0. To Talley, J., Kill, by name, a head's share: 1.8. To the book: 7.2.*"

"That's seven and eight-tenths with my name on it," said Jude. He was by the dresser, as ever. "In one autumn. A draft board will think that I've been robbing a van."

"*Held: 26.53. Sealed, medical, W: 0.8. Opened once; see the 6th.*" Kellan blotted it. "That adds. I've done it three ways."

"It can't be made up," said Anwen. "The reserve. I asked at the ash office. There isn't a pinch of plain ward-trace to be bought in Meridian; it's all had the hair since the thaw. So it's eight-tenths, and it stays eight-tenths until somebody kills something quiet." She did not sound sorry. "I'd sooner have it short and know what it was for."

"I've a line," said Jude.

They looked at him. He had never proposed one. He was on the book by name, for a share, and had been careful since the seventh month to ask for nothing else.

"Stopgate. The top of it. There are four streets on boxes off drum twenty-nine, for a month, since a plank split. It was nobody's plank. It was under the knot next to mine." He scratched his jaw. "A dram, at par, to the wardens' account. I asked last week what ash was for, and a man told me. I'd like to have done it once on purpose."

"That was my line," said Perrin. "In the seventh month. I'd like it known that I'm not jealous. I'm flattered. It's a new feeling and I don't trust it."

Anwen wrote it. *Returned at par to the wardens of Stopgate, for the boxes on drum twenty-nine: 1.0. Held: 25.53.*

"Saltmere," said Kellan. "The office hasn't posted a warrant for it. Nobody from this Circuit has been sent to the coast in eleven years. It was two a head to a regional qualifier, and it'll not be less." He did the sum aloud, since he could not help it. "Five heads. Ten, at the least. We've five-and-twenty. I've never before had a figure come out with something over."

"Five?" said Jude.

"If you'll stand. By name. On your three things."

"I'll stand. I'd want to see the sea." The chipped tooth showed. "And Renn's feet on a strange floor. I've a notion that they'll fall in love again."

"They're cured."

"Nobody's cured. Ask Aske."

Kiva had the card in her coat. She had carried it since six on Friday.

She laid it on the table, on the green cover, and read it to them. She read every word, down to *No seat is promised.* Then she told them the two things that she had asked for in the yard, and what she would do if they said no.

"Six nights," said Kellan. "Two in a month, for three months. It costs the book nothing. If we want a floor on one of them, she's ours; it's written." He turned the card over, to see whether there was anything on the back. "I can't find the catch. I've looked for it as I'd look for one of Adlam's."

"I mind," said Perrin.

He had his hands flat on the table. He was strapped to the armpits again, and sat like a man in a barrel.

"I mind that it's Sorn, who's had us in brackets since the fifth month. I mind that it's you, again, with a card. I've looked under both of those, and there's nothing there that would bear weight." He drew what breath he could. "I've a place in my Hall's second team from the new year, in my own colors. I've a figure cut in a stone. So I'm signing, and I'd like it noted that I minded properly, and at length, and that it was only habit."

"What's behind it?" said Anwen.

"A hem. Pike. And my mouth shut until the bag's sealed."

"For three months."

"Yes."

Anwen considered that for some time.

"I'd pay to watch," she said. She signed the back of a chartered company's card, in brown ink, which nobody at the ash office had a rule for. The others signed under her.

Mara came up the shop stair as they were blotting it. She had eleven sheets under her arm, tied with tape, and she carried them in her left hand.

"That's for the coast boat on Monday," she said. "*How the Ferren bank was taken out of step, with what was got wrong first.* I've put Rusk's firebreak in it, by his leave. He said that if I left it out, he'd come and write it in himself." She laid the bundle on the dresser and put the brass weight on it. Then she looked at the table, and the book, and four names on a slate-blue card. "I'm told that nobody's to ask me whether she ought. I notice that nobody has."

"I asked them," said Kiva.

"So I heard. Through a floor." Something happened at the corner of Mara's mouth. "It took a good deal longer than telling them would have."

---

Orla Dane had moved her crate.

It was not far. It was a pace to the north, out of the line of the south row. A watch officer had pointed out, quite civilly, that she had been sitting on a casualty road for one-and-twenty years, and she had not been able to find an answer to it.

It was Sunday evening. The yard was empty. She had the lamp on its hook.

There was a bar on the bottom wall now. It hung on two iron hooks that a fitter had let into the brick, at the height of a man's shoulder, an ash pole as thick as a wrist. Under it stood the post, black and split and banded. At the post's foot, where a cross had been scratched for her by a girl's heel all summer, lay a square of iron with a ring in it.

*You're no help,* she had said to that post. She had said it for years.

She took the card out of her coat again.

It had come on Friday, by the coast boat. It was a plain card, of the cheap sort that pump-fitters used for their accounts, and it had four lines on it in a round, rather childish hand that she would have known in the dark.

*Your letter was four lines. So is this.*

*I am a pump-fitter. Our harbour bank went into step on the 17th, at twenty past two. I knew what it was before anybody told me. I knew it with the hand, the bad one.*

*Tell the one who writes everything down that I should like to read it.*

*J.H.*

She had not written to be answered. She had said so, to a Placement Chair, across a desk, and had been rather proud of the sentence.

She found that it had been a lie of the tidying kind. She had wanted this for twenty years. She had only arranged matters so that she should not have to know it.

He had not said that he forgave her. She examined the card for that, as a wall-holder goes over a wall for a thin place, and it was not there. He had said that he fitted pumps. He had said that the hand was bad. And he had asked for something, plainly, of a girl whom he had never seen, since she had a thing that he wanted.

*I did not know to teach you that.*

It seemed that somebody had taught him to ask.

There were feet in the lane. There were a good many, and they were small.

She put the card away.

---

They had sent the juniors. There were fourteen of them, in their grey smocks, in a huddle by the first shed, and with them a bursar's clerk holding a proof sheet as though it might go off.

Kiva had come by the side gate with Anwen. She had not known that there would be children.

"The tenth edition," said Orla. She did not get up. "They've set the lane. Seven columns. They've put the seventh back, in the same type as the other six, and I'm told that it cost the printer a night's sleep to make it fit." She took the sheet from the clerk and held it out. "The bursar wants to know whether anything should be added. He asked me. I said that I wasn't the one who'd found it out twice."

Kiva took it in her right hand. Anwen, without being asked, held the lamp.

It was all there. *The lane with the crooked posts. At Meridian, under the south wall, on the line of the hill drain.* It had the nine posts, and the sluice, and the bar. *Call the turn before the eighth, aloud, and say who has the bar.* It had a collarbone, and a cellar in the Hythe.

And at the right, in print, with two initials that nobody in Meridian could put a face to:

*Why: because the road isn't straight. S.V.*

She had thought about that line a good deal in ten days. She had thought that when she understood it she would have something to say.

She did. It was about a plan that had been tidied by a helpful clerk, and a post that had been aimed and called crooked, and a girl who had gone east in the Pit and had nearly gone east again. It was about there being room on a bar for two. It had a shine on it that one could have shaved by.

She did not write any of it.

"There's a pencil in my coat," she said. "The left pocket. I can't get it out with this hand; the pocket's got a button."

"I know which pocket," said Anwen. She got it out.

Kiva laid the proof on the lid of Orla's crate. She held it flat with the heel of her left hand, and the last two fingers. The first two lay on the paper, and told her that there was paper.

She did not touch the last column. It had been right for five hundred years. It had only wanted the other six.

She wrote in the fifth, where it said *Went wrong,* under the collarbone.

*2680. The mark was called east, since east had served once before. East was the drain. The step was tried first, and it said so.*

And in the sixth, under *Second try:*

*Called north, aloud, before. Said back by two. Three others had the corner after six. Two had the bar. One had the count, from a chair. K.R.*

She read it over. It did not look like wisdom. It looked like a meter reading.

"You've not put what it cost," said Orla.

"It's in Vane's ledger. And Aske's card. And the green book." She gave the pencil back to Anwen. "I'd be putting it in a fourth place so as to have it where students could see."

"That's a reason not to."

"It's the reason I've got."

Orla looked at the two lines for some time. Her crooked face did something complicated, and settled.

"I asked you in the spring to show me six," she said. "I was rather pleased with it. I'd no notion what I was asking for." She folded the proof along its crease and gave it to the clerk. "That goes as it is. Tell the bursar that there's a hand in the fifth column that isn't a founder's, and that if he tidies it, I shall come up his stair."

The clerk fled.

The juniors had been edging down the lane. They had got as far as the fourth post. The sandy one with the plait was in front, and she had her eyes on the bar.

"Miss Dane says that we're to run it," she said. "Dark. From the sheds. She says that it's in the manual again." She looked at Kiva. "You said that you didn't know why it was crooked. I asked you. You said that you'd wondered for four years."

"I had."

"Do you know now?"

It was the plainest question that Kiva had been asked since a watch officer had held up a slate.

She could have told her. It would have taken about a minute. The child would have nodded, and gone round the post by her little sideways step, exactly as before, with a sentence in her head in place of a blank.

*Then they should go and find out,* somebody had once said, of students who laughed. Kiva had never heard it. She had only been walking toward it since she was thirteen.

"I know what it's for," she said. "I'd sooner you told me. Run it. From the sheds, in the dark. There are two things to say aloud before you reach the eighth post, and I'll give you the first. *Turn.* And which way."

"What's the second?"

"Who has the bar."

"*I* haven't got the bar."

"No. So you'll have to say who has. Before you get there. That's the whole of it."

The child thought about that. She looked along the fourteen of them.

"There's got to be two," she said slowly. "It's too long for one. Somebody'd have to be at the other end." Her face cleared. "Bettony. You're tall. You're the bar with me. Say it back."

"I'm the bar with you," said a long thin girl, in a voice of doom.

They went up to the sheds in a body, arguing about it.

Kiva walked back along the lane to the first shed, and stood under the lamp, where Orla had sat for twenty-one years. Anwen came with her. Neither of them stood at the post.

"You could have told her," said Anwen.

"I had it ready."

"I know. I watched you put it in your pocket." She was not smiling. She had the look that she wore when a wall had been gone over and had no thin place in it. She put the pencil back in the left pocket, and did up the button, without comment, as one does up a child's. "I told Mace on Thursday. About coming off a join. He'd it on his board already. He'd written, *Said so as she went.* He's left my name where it was."

"I knew that he would."

"You didn't. You hoped. They aren't the same."

"No."

The lamp hissed. At the far end of the lane, somebody small was counting three, aloud, for courage.

Kiva looked down at her left wrist. The cuff had come undone again. A button wanted an edge found, and a hole, and one thread told from two.

"Anwen," she said. "My cuff."

She said it before she had tried. It was not hard to say. That was what surprised her. She had hated it for a fortnight, and Pike had promised her a year.

"Give it here," said Anwen.

They came.

They came out of the dark at the sheds' end in a pounding, uneven line, fourteen pairs of boots on packed dirt, with nothing lit. They went by the lamp like a flock of starlings. A small, breathless, sandy voice went up ahead of them.

"*Turn! Left! Bar's me and Bettony!*"

"*Bar's me!*" That was the voice of doom. "*Say it back!*"

"*Turn left! Bar's them!*" said twelve others, more or less together.

Not one of them struck the post.

Two of them reached up to the wall. Kiva heard the ash pole come off its hooks, and a great deal of advice. She heard it go home in the socket. She did not hear it turn. They had been told that a gate was not to be shut on a dry night, and for once in the history of that School a junior had believed a thing because she had been given the reason with it.

"That's the first time that lane's been run to the bar since 2390," said Orla. She had her palm flat on the dirt beside her crate, as a woman feels for a tram. "I've sat here one-and-twenty years. I never thought to hear it."

Kiva did not answer.

She was looking over the bottom wall. It was a clear night, with a frost coming. Beyond thirty feet of brick the long hill went down, straight as a rule, with a lamp at every twentieth pace, to the Brickfields road and the river and the dark.

There was a card in her coat from a company, with *either of us* on the front of it and four names on the back. There was a line on a board about a Measure at Saltmere, in the third month. There was a pump-fitter on the south coast who knew a thing by his bad hand, and wanted to read what she had written down. Somewhere beyond all of them, a keeper whom she had never heard of had a book with a remarks column.

It was two hundred miles to the coast. She had seen the map on the wall of the watch room. The road went round the Sag, and doubled at a ferry, and followed a river that had never in its life gone where a clerk would have drawn it.

Down the lane the juniors had found that the bar would lift two of them off the ground if four more sat on the other end.

"Your cuff's done," said Anwen. "You may have your arm back."

Kiva took it back.

She looked once at the post. It stood a pace out from its row, black against the brick, with a bar through it and six children hanging from the bar. It leaned to the left, as it always had.

It was not listening for anything. She could see that now. It had only ever been pointing at the place where the road turned, for as long as there had been somebody to say so aloud.

## FORMULA ALIGNMENT — EDITORIAL, NOT BLIND

# O'Connor 1.3.0 — numerical author formula

# Applying the owner's numerical craft formula

The owner explicitly selected
`/Users/drive/penname/research/ironprince-craft-formula.md` to govern this book.
Use its craft instructions more or less exactly, including the numbers. These
are active design targets for the planning model and Opus/Fable author, not merely
editorial observations. The compiler embeds sections 2–6 and 8–9 verbatim below
these instructions and records the source hash so runs can be compared.

Let the sentence-length distribution, short/long sentence shares, paragraph
rhythm and Flesch reading targets influence the actual prose. Let the POV shares,
front-loaded progression vocabulary and distributed character-development rates
influence how the book is built. Preserve section 8's secondary/use-to-taste
status; do not turn its explicitly excluded filler/repetition tics into quotas.

Write original scenes in open-range movements of six or more chapters. The formula constrains the intended
shape and cadence; the author still chooses conversations, tactics, discoveries
and how events unfold. Do not reduce this brief to “clear writing with progression.”
The owner names the cast. No source novel quotations, character substitutions or
scene-by-scene replication belong in the manuscript.

Aim at the supplied distributions across a movement and the cumulative book.
Book-third teaching proportions and book-level POV proportions need not be met
independently by each chapter. Do not stop after each sentence or chapter to run a
gate. Write the full movement, then compare target versus observed results in the
existing editorial pass and make a focused same-author repair for material drift.
Do not silently broaden tolerances or drop inconvenient targets.

Keep the source's own caveats: pause-derived paragraph/scene measures are
directional proxies, and development counts are heuristic. Their adoption as
design targets does not establish that they accurately measure the printed book.
The full source-book audit remains unfinished. Keep source values such as ~1.7x
and ~950 words unchanged; the known arithmetic/denominator questions belong in
the audit, not a silent rewrite of the owner's chosen prompt. If competing targets
cannot be reconciled, report the conflict rather than fabricating a passing score.

This prompt guides output; it does not train model weights or guarantee exact
statistical compliance. Compliance must be measured after prose exists.

Source: /Users/drive/penname/research/ironprince-craft-formula.md
Source SHA-256: 9f97e2f225c0bcf61d2e922719153fcf00e3b029f7b1552f213888d33130c0b8

The following craft sections (2–6, 8–9) are included verbatim. Sections 1 and 7's legal discussion is not drafting guidance. Use original names, world, systems, events and phrasing; the owner names the cast.

## 2. Sentence rhythm

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Mean sentence length | 14.6 words |
| Median sentence length | 11 words |
| Spread (population stdev) | ~26 words — wide, meaning short declaratives sit right next to long compound-complex sentences, not clustered near the mean |
| Share of very short sentences (≤5 words) | 27.7% |
| Share of very long sentences (≥40 words) | 3.3% |

**Read:** roughly 1 sentence in 4 is a hard, short beat (often a single clause landing a
punch after a longer setup) — this is the "short sentence for impact" technique, not
uniform mid-length prose. Target this ratio, not just the mean; a manuscript that hits mean
14.6 but has no short-sentence spikes will not read the same.

## 3. Paragraph and scene rhythm (ASR-inferred proxy)

Whisper transcripts carry no typographic paragraph marks — this section is inferred from
pause gaps between narrated speech segments, not a count of the print manuscript's actual
paragraph breaks. Treat as directional, not exact.

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Inferred paragraph length (pause ≥0.6s) | median 18 words, mean 26.8 words — short, frequent beats |
| Inferred scene/section length (pause ≥1.6s) | mean ~950 words between scene-level breaks, ~8.7 scene breaks per 10k words |

**Read:** short paragraphs are the default texture, with scenes running roughly 900–1000
words before a beat change (POV settle, location change, or time-skip). Avoid long
unbroken expository blocks; break early and often.

## 4. Progression-mechanic ("change frequency") callout rate

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Progression/stat-vocabulary density | ~58 hits per 10k words (rank, growth-analog, spec, level-up, stat-block language) |
| Placement | front-loaded — the opening third of the book carries roughly 1.7x the progression-callout density of the middle and closing thirds (900 vs. 471 vs. 514 hits, by third) |

**Read:** the system gets *established* early and hard, then recedes into the background
as character and plot take over — it does not stay evenly dense through the whole book.
Build your own progression system's vocabulary (do not reuse his axis names — see §7), but
match this front-loaded shape: teach the mechanic aggressively in the first third, then let
it surface only at genuine turning points afterward.

## 5. Character-development beat cadence

Methodology: named-character mention within ~120 characters of a reflection/interiority
marker ("realized," "decided," "for the first time," "swore," "no longer," etc.) — a
heuristic count, not a scene-by-scene read.

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Protagonist development-moment rate | 4.5 per 10k words |
| Protagonist distribution across thirds | roughly even (41 / 56 / 50 — a slight middle-third bump, not backloaded) |
| Supporting-cast development-moment rate | an order of magnitude lower per character (0.1–0.7 per 10k words each), spread across 8–10 named characters rather than concentrated in one deuteragonist |

**Read:** growth beats for the lead land steadily throughout, not saved up for a finale —
and the supporting cast gets *many* small beats each rather than one character getting a
big secondary arc. If your story gives one best-friend character 80% of the secondary
development budget, that's a different shape than this book's.

## 6. POV structure

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Protagonist POV share | ~87% of chapter-word-volume (combining his given-name and surname references) |
| Secondary-character POV/focus share | ~13%, spread across 4–5 named characters at 2.5–3.7% each — brief cutaways, not sustained alternating-POV chapters |

**Read:** this is single-POV-dominant with occasional short cutaway scenes to a handful of
other named characters, not a true multi-POV structure. If you want to signal "feels like
Iron Prince," don't build alternating-chapter dual POV — keep the lead's POV overwhelming
and let other characters get glimpses, not chapters.

## 8. Supporting craft signals (secondary, use to taste)

- **Filler-word discipline:** "that" runs hot (92.1 per 10k words) — a known, common genre
  tic, not a target to imitate; if anything, tighten below this. Other fillers ("just,"
  "almost," "felt," "seemed") sit in the 5–25-per-10k band — moderate, not aggressively
  self-edited, not sloppy either.
- **Adverb density:** ~161 -ly adverbs per 10k words — fairly high; this is a book that
  lets adverbs carry tone rather than insisting on pure show-don't-tell. Not a craft flaw
  to correct, a genre-register signal to optionally match.
- **Dialogue-tag (reporting-verb) density:** ~41 per 10k words — moderate dialogue presence,
  consistent with an action/interiority-forward book rather than a dialogue-driven one.
- **Combat-vocabulary density:** ~22 per 10k words (strike/block/dodge/blade/impact/etc.) —
  present throughout, not just in set-piece fight chapters.
- **Readability:** Flesch Reading Ease 72.3, Flesch-Kincaid grade 6.8 — accessible YA/adult
  crossover register, short-to-medium sentence construction, low jargon-density outside the
  progression-vocabulary spikes.
- **Repetition discipline:** common connective 3-grams ("out of the," "one of the," "for a
  moment") sit at 2–4 per 10k words — normal English-prose background rate, not a
  distinctive tic to replicate. Don't chase these; they're noise, not signature.

---

## 9. One-paragraph brief for the drafting agent

> Write single-POV-dominant (~85–90% lead-character screen time), short-paragraph,
> pause-heavy prose (median paragraph ~18 words, scenes breaking roughly every 900–1000
> words). Mix short punch sentences (~28% of all sentences ≤5 words) against longer
> compound-complex ones (mean 14.6 words, wide spread) rather than settling into uniform
> mid-length sentences. Establish your progression system hard and often in the first
> third of the book, then let it recede to occasional high-stakes surfacing afterward. Land
> a protagonist growth/realization beat roughly every 2,200 words, spread evenly across the
> book rather than saved for the climax, and give 8–10 supporting characters small
> development beats each rather than concentrating growth in one deuteragonist. Moderate
> dialogue-tag and adverb use; accessible ~7th-grade reading level; combat vocabulary
> present in the background throughout, not confined to fight scenes. Every name, every
> system-label, every scene: yours, invented, original.

## NUMERICAL REVIEW

# Numerical formula alignment — after the movement

This is an informed editorial pass, not a blind cold read. Read the supplied
formula and full movement, then report target, observed result, method, coverage
and uncertainty. Keep numerical alignment separate from reader scores.

- Sentence mean, median, population standard deviation; shares ≤5 and ≥40 words.
- Written paragraph mean/median and actual scene/section boundaries. Compare with
  the requested targets, but do not equate typography with ASR pause segmentation.
- Flesch Reading Ease and Flesch-Kincaid grade. Identify the sentence tokenizer and
  syllable estimator; proper names/system terms can affect estimates.
- Progression vocabulary hits per 10,000 words, and placement by equal word-count
  book thirds once available. Define a project-specific lexicon before counting;
  do not borrow the source novel's names. An incomplete book cannot confirm the
  full front-loading ratio. Report provisional cumulative coverage instead.
- Lead development moments and supporting-cast distribution: cite actual choices
  or realizations and distinguish manually identified beats from regex proxies.
- POV word share: identify viewpoint segments, not the most frequently mentioned
  name. Track the lead and secondary viewpoints against the book-level allocation.
- Secondary signals: -ly words, reporting verbs, combat terms, fillers and repeated
  phrases. Publish lexicons and counting definitions. A suffix count is not proof
  that every matched word is an adverb. Preserve optional/non-target distinctions.

Use reproducible counts when tooling exists. Otherwise mark a metric UNMEASURED;
never estimate a precise score by intuition. Save the method or command with the
report so later runs use the same definitions. Inspect ASR reference limitations
without discarding the owner's chosen drafting targets.

Locate meaningful departures, especially uniform sentence lengths, missing short
beats, reading level drift, dense paragraphs, late teaching or a coequal second
lead replacing the requested POV shape. Consolidate the most important repairs
with continuity findings into the existing bounded author repair. Protect exact
owner text and successful scenes. Do not iterate indefinitely to hit decimals or
hide irreconcilable targets. Bring consequential formula conflicts to the owner.

## PROJECT EVIDENCE — STATE_LEDGER.md

# Kindling 2680 opening state ledger

Status: opening locks resolved 2026-10-08. Ready for the prologue and first
eight-chapter continuous prose run.

## Prologue and opening day

- Year 2680, during the six-hundredth-anniversary season of the Fall.
- The prologue's final historical snapshot is 2676: **Kiva Rowan**, thirteen,
  reaches the Kindling uninjured and opens all three known doors.
- Chapter One begins four years later. Kiva is seventeen and seeking collegiate
  placement for the 2680 season.
- Kiva lives with her aunt and legal guardian, **Mara Rowan**, in Meridian. Mara
  repairs municipal instruments and reconciles their paper records.
- The Satori disk reached Kiva through a sealed Home Line appointment by its prior
  bearer, not through blood descent. Kiva knows it is old and entrusted; she does
  not know its full public history or origin.
- Meridian's schools expect one first door and have procedures for unusual strength,
  but no accepted process for three simultaneous traditions.

## Knowledge boundaries

- General public: knows the Fall, Kindling, ranks, schools, Riftspawn, and ordinary
  filter language as history/science appropriate to the final world-state choice.
- Students: know institutional versions of Home Line history, not necessarily the
  surviving founder records.
- Orla Dane: knows the disk family and at least one suppressed prior anomaly.
- Kiva: does not know the disk's true origin, the full filter theory, or why
  all three doors opened.
- Kiva remembers the 2676 fever dream in sensory fragments: Glory-level figures
  converging, the sky/filter absent, pure presence pressing into the world, and every
  ash-lit thing responding. She does not know the later name **Unroofed Sky**, whether
  the dream predicts anything, or that older Glory accounts overlap it.
- Orla knows there are disputed historical fever accounts but does not begin Book One
  with proof that Kiva's dream matches them.
- Reader: sees evidence before explanation; no prologue solves the mystery.

## Physical and progression state

- All three doors open at Kindling, but none is mastered.
- The event leaves three fine, asymmetrical heat-lines from Kiva's sternum toward
  her left shoulder. They remain visible and become tender when the doors compete.
- The disk warms once, only after Kiva completes the doors in a correct deliberate
  sequence. It never instructs her or grants technique.
- The disk can amplify or carry the Unroofed Sky fever dream, but it cannot trigger
  on demand, answer questions, or supply a combat/engineering solution.
- Initial classification is incomplete or contradictory, not a secret top rank.

## Social state

- Kiva has a real home/family obligation in Mara's repair-and-records work.
- Anwen Pryce, Perrin Cade, and Kellan Renn have independent placements and
  goals before meeting the protagonist.
- Institutions have defensible reasons to fear a category they cannot safely test.

## Institutional and world locks

- Campus compact: **the Meridian Compact**.
- Mixed-path college: **the Crooked Line School**.
- Resilience/body college: **Stonehand Hall**.
- Medical, Ward, Sustain, and ash-engineering college: **Wren House**.
- Collegiate competition: **the Meridian Collegiate Circuit**; final regional
  event: **the Regional Measure**. Events include individual Measures, team lanes,
  ashcraft trials, rescue/extraction problems, and controlled Riftspawn engagements.
- The collegiate curriculum includes a staged field license: contained spawn anatomy
  and kill drills, supervised Handful kills, live-rift recovery, then higher-yield team
  responses. Book One devotes several connected chapters to Kiva's team learning how
  to kill Riftspawn and recover the ash cleanly before the first major live response.
- Above college are **chartered rift companies**: salaried professional teams that
  fight Riftspawn, close or contain rifts, rescue civilians, protect infrastructure,
  and recover ash. Standings reward closure, lives saved, protected infrastructure,
  clean ash, and rule compliance—not kills alone.
- Book One is regional. Book Two expands toward national competition and company
  politics. Book Three becomes international as the filter and ash grid destabilize.
- Mature hardened regional technology and scheduled wormhole stations exist. Travel
  is expensive and bounded; interplanetary normality does not.
- Classification uses physical instruments, projected tables, and human rulings.
  There is no private retinal or software-like status overlay.
- **Draw** is ash spent on standard work divided by that task's book/bench expectation:
  `1.0` is standard and lower is more efficient. A contested four-minute hold uses a
  book expectation of twelve hundredths of a dram.

## Ash-electric civilization and Glory

- Refined ash is the dominant finite energy source. **Ash induction** converts a
  controlled ash reaction into ordinary current for lights, pumps, heat,
  communications, medicine, workshops, transit, arenas, and rift sensing.
- Ash does not create free energy. Cells are graded, metered, consumed, reconciled,
  and vulnerable to contamination or runaway.
- **Ashcraft** blends magic and ash: a trained soul supplies pattern, intention,
  and control; ownerless ash supplies most of the fuel. Different traditions shape,
  contain, conduct, or sustain the reaction differently.
- Modern cells continue running after a Kindled operator starts and tunes them, but
  still require maintenance, replacement ash, and emergency shutdown.
- At **Glory**, a rare master can impress stable patterns at civic or industrial
  scale. Glory does not make ash infinite; it expands safe throughput, duration,
  precision, and the complexity of blended systems.
- That capability produces ash-power industrial dynasties as well as school orders
  whose masters pursue near-monastic devotion to one tradition. Some orders reject
  corporate ownership; some industry houses sponsor them; some Glory masters move
  between both worlds.
- Pro rift companies feed the ash grid. This creates prestige and public service,
  but also incentives to overhunt, hide risk, manipulate ash grades, or protect
  wealthy districts first.
- A sanctioned kill produces a recorded yield. Civic power, casualty reserve, school,
  and team shares are removed in that order. Student shares fund training cells,
  metered combat techniques, equipment, travel, and medical reserve; they are not
  unmetered personal treasure.
- Ash is recorded by **yield** (Handful, Flask, Barrel, Well), **grade** (purity and
  stability), and **resonance** (which patterns can use that spawn expression safely).
  A large dirty yield may be worth less than a small stable one.
- Killing does not grant rank automatically. Real combat can deepen capacity because
  the practitioner works at the edge of owned control and must later reproduce the
  lesson. Ash funds the next threshold; training makes the threshold survivable.
- Filter thinning increases both human potential and Riftspawn pressure. The opening
  book presents this first as unusually capable students and an unusually varied rift
  season. Later books prove the common Glory-pressure cause.

## First-season lasting cost

During the high-stakes regional sequence Kiva will permanently lose fine sensation
in the first two fingers of her left hand. Healing closes the injury but does not
restore exact touch. The cost is planted before it occurs and changes how she grips,
trains, and asks for help. Rank cannot erase it.

## Movement Three carried state (chapters 17–24)

Recorded 2026-10-11 after the consolidated same-author repair. **Passed by a separate
same-model informed targeted recheck; no second prose repair is justified.** The manuscript governs; full detail is in
`provenance/MOVEMENT-003-CONTINUITY.md`.

  1st**; the investment vote **Saturday the 2nd**; the Old Yard **Sunday the 3rd**.
  Movement Four opens after Sunday the 3rd.
- **The sequence.** Kiva has completed one correct deliberate two-door sequence, Stride
  into stance: about nine seconds of stance, 0.31 of a dram (0.02 + 0.26 + 0.03), Draw
  near twelve. It was done under a recorded Home Line field direction, after the event
  was struck, outside her certificate. It is not certified. Orla's standard is "Show me
  six"; Team Seven has funded six sanctioned tries on three written conditions (a

- **The disk** warmed once, on the 29th. Kiva **first felt it after** she had shut the

- **Institutional.** The Circuit's book now reads "A gate shall be left lit" (from the

  10:24 and about forty seconds) and the unread 2446 margin; she has kept them from the

- **Lane calls.** "Half!" names the side that earned it; "Down!" names the side that lost

- **Calendar.** The month is still unnamed and has thirty days. Movement Four runs from

- **Knowledge.** The 2446 margin is read (a sister of the Even Hand asked at the wicket

  pulled her from a culvert with a hand shut on something at her throat, and that she will
  burn a card of two words; the words are not said. Mara does not yet know what the bearer's
  book records about her. Nobody connects the stepped ash, the T-0 entries, the notch, the
  disk and the dream.
- **Words.** "A crossing" is the Home Line's older word for a sequence (ledger entry of 2471,
  Tansy Wold). "A stack" is Kellan's and Orla's word for two doors open together. "Stepped"
  and "the Step" are Halden's; "counting ash" was the Wren House's earlier name.
- **Names and pronouns introduced (author's choice, pending owner review).** Lettice
  Arkwright ("Reader," she); Mr. Adlam (he); Pike, Nairn and Ruddock of Graywater (he);
  Warden Cobb (she); Dulcie (she) and Stott (he); Brannock (he); Tansy Wold (she); M. Gill
  (he); Pettit (a name only). Perrin's father and the sister of the Even Hand are unnamed.

## Movement Five carried state (chapters 33–40)

Recorded 2026-10-11 from the first draft, by its author, directly after the run, and
reconciled the same day to the consolidated same-author repair. **Repaired and targeted-rechecked;
accepted as the Movement Six handoff.** The manuscript governs; full detail is in
`provenance/MOVEMENT-005-CONTINUITY.md` (its section 0 lists what the repair changed). Only
what the prose shows is entered here.

- **Calendar.** The months are numbered on the page from this movement: the Pit was the fifth
  month, the Weighbridge the sixth; Movement Five runs from Sunday the 1st of the seventh to
  Monday the 26th of the ninth. Each still has thirty days. The watch sat on the 1st of the
  seventh; the Chair heard Selka Dray on the 2nd and all parties on the bag on the 3rd. The
  Regional Measure was Friday the 16th and Saturday the 17th of the ninth month.
- **The six.** Complete. Four more crossings at 0.31 each (the 2nd, 10th, 17th and 24th of the
  seventh month; the second of them on the Circuit's Great Floor), one a week, the hand seen
  the next morning. The practice glass ran 0.94, 0.63, 0.32, 0.01; the hundredth went into the
  team's next entry. Orla's certificate now adds: one crossing, Ember to still, low, to a
  mark, declared to a count, on a sanctioned mast read by the Chair's reader, one in seven
  days; not certified: any stack, any third door, anything she has not seen six times.
- **Three doors.** Stated on the page before any attempt: a competition (all open, fighting,
  nobody choosing), a stack (all open, agreeing; soft, cheap, and open to anything near), a
  sequence (each shut before the next opens, with two gaps). Kiva's order is hand, Ember,
  still ("catch, carry, plant"), priced at 0.59 whole. Five tries on a sanctioned mast in the
  eighth month: a jam (0.11), a hand-to-Ember crossing (0.30), an *off* (0.31), a stack at the
  second transition that she called herself (0.39), and **one clean (0.59, the 29th).** One
  more on the floor of the Pit on the 17th of the ninth month, under a recorded field
  direction, after the event was struck. **Not certified, not classified, and by the Chair's
  written entry "not to be asked of her."**
- **Body. The cost is paid.** The first two fingers of the left hand have **permanently lost
  fine sensation**: they bend, grip and report pressure; they do not tell edge from flat, hot
  from cold, or one thread from two (Healer Aske, the 24th of the ninth month). The rest of
  the hand returned within two days. Ward-trace ash and rest are both ruled out as remedies.
  It followed three choices shown on the page: declining the Order's year of rest; spending
  the week's one crossing in a lane on the 16th; and making two gaps the next day against a
  card that read *No go before the 23rd,* with the price read out first by her healer and her
  foreman. She catches with the first two fingers held clear ("passengers"), hands the cord to
  Pru Finch from the first turn, and needs help with a button.
- **The disk.** Cold throughout. The Home Line watch heard her entry; a recall for cause was
  moved and lost two to five. Written by the watch: the plate is held of the Line and borne by
  K. Rowan; it is not equipment, an instrument or evidence; no hand but the bearer's is laid
  on it without a vote; the bearer writes in the fourth column within the week after any
  crossing. One earlier fourth-column line in the old record hand has been read: **Thirza Bly,
  bearer from 2466: "Warm, once, after the first crossing. I have crossed nine times since.
  Cold."** No cause, door or use is given. The School asked to borrow the plate and was
  answered from the fourth edition.
- **The dream.** No recurrence, and no fever on the night of the 17th. The Order of the Even
  Hand offered a year as a guest of its house, one door, every other shut; Kiva declined and
  Mara left the offer "on the latch."
- **Certificate and conditions.** Place, division and sponsor unchanged. Added by the Chair on
  the 2nd of the seventh month: no second door on any Circuit floor except a crossing declared
  to a count and read at the time from a sanctioned mast by the office's reader; a stack so
  read is an incident; not at the Bench; recovery only on any floor with a contained rift. The
  mast condition is now general, for any candidate with more than one door, under Selka
  Dray's name. No crossing counts unless the tape shows the cliff; Noa Bexley is reader to the
  office; Orla keeps her standing.
- **Stepped ash.** The Weighbridge bag: under three seals (the Works', the wardens', the
  Chair's) in the Wren House cold room; entered as made in Weighbridge Street; the street
  advanced 56.5 drams of plain stock by the casualty reserve; one dram each to the reference
  shelf and the Halden technical office; the patent claim entered and sent to the Compact's
  court, not ruled on. **New Circuit rules:** any catcher may red-tag a bag on stated grounds
  ("sufficient to withhold… not sufficient to prove"); no ash above I-trace on any Circuit
  floor; every Regional dram combed at the gate.
- **The Regional Measure.** Sixteen fours, four boards, each marked by place (sixteen down to
  one) and added. A lane carry scores three for the litter home and one for a down. Lane: Seven beat Old Four 8–7 and
  lost to Halden 8–6 (Kellan touched Kiva in the gap at the top of a declared crossing; a
  down; the crossing begun and not landed; not a stack; no incident; **an unlanded crossing still
  spends the hand and the week's go**). Bench: Seven third
  without Kiva. Carry: sixth. The Call was struck at 2:21 on the 17th and closed at 3:10 by
  the Home Line. **Result, read on three boards: 1. Halden License. 2. Old Four (att.). 3.
  Team Twenty-Eight. 4. T7 (att.).**
- **The hold.** During the Call four skinned husks, two of them unordered, stood a square
  over the Pit's race with a white cord run from each to the next and a tear inside it,
  outside the lattice. Lowen Brask, back from a summer on Home Line holds, named it. Selka
  Dray cut one corner inside her step on her catcher's word and was pinned by the cord (left
  leg above the knee, the bone, and a burn of cold; under the cord about a quarter of an hour).
  The string in the tear rose a knot to about a hundred counts. Kiva took the cord off her with
  the three-door sequence, carried the corner four paces east along the line of the hold's south
  side, so that all of Dray but the pinned left leg and both carriers stayed on the open side of the wall, and held it ten counts; Perrin and Wilf Marten got Dray out;
  Kellan, Jude Talley and Gower cut the other three posts on Kiva's word as she released;
  Anwen called a four-ward student gate that took the new string and held its face about forty
  minutes until the tear was shut; the closing crew closed
  the tear; Rhea Sorn and Halden's foreman cut two strings; each team bagged a rung. Rhea Sorn's shouted stand to Twenty-Eight came as the catcher's hand
  fell, too late to govern, and is on the slate. Nobody
  on the page says why spawn stood a hold. The Home Line has suspended held tears.
- **Instrument evidence.** At the Pit every lamp on the dead rim showed a point of light once
  (the keeper's second T-0 on that ring) and all sixteen pens notched eleven seconds after
  Kiva's second cliff, both at the instant she said *back*, on hands lit and dark alike; Mr. Adlam of the Halden technical office saw it and has written so
  under his own name. No notch followed the crossing on the Great Floor. Unexplained.
- **The grid.** The Ferren pump bank (twenty-four cells; the Municipal Works', not Halden's)
  went into step when the hold stood
  and **has not come out**, though its link was open; the pumps have not dropped a stroke.
  The Step is spreading along the Brickfields trunk at about a drum every two days (nineteen,
  twenty, twenty-one), with nine drums left before the Crooked Line School's core house.
  Nobody knows how to take a bank out of step.
- **Team Seven.** Fourth at the Regional; **still second step, attached** to Rhea Sorn, who
  has made no offer. Book: 2.0 sealed medical (W), never opened; **held 5.62; 18.0 credited
  from the Pit's pooled clean ash** (24.0 to each team; 6.0 by name to Jude Talley). Spent in
  this movement: 1.24 on four crossings, 2.1 on three Kennel floors, 1.0 returned at par to
  Weighbridge Street for the Pellow's boxes, 1.29 on three whole three-door tries (of a line
  of 1.8 that Perrin first refused and then proposed with his own brake). Seven's own rung
  from the Pit is red-tagged, stepped, on Kiva's tag. Jude stood the Regional as Kill by
  name; Anwen stood the Hold on her own terms; Kellan cut for Halden until the floor was
  struck and then declared for Seven under rule nineteen.
- **People.** Mara passed her re-sit on the 7th of the ninth month (Grade Two restored, the
  left her reading hand) and is reading the bank for Examiner Rusk. Anwen finished Mace's
  line without a dip and has a relief's place on the night van's ward line for next summer;
  she is "done minding" and has "not forgotten." Kellan has not answered Halden's offer of its
  yard for the winter. Perrin has been passed to the shoulder since the end of the eighth month, lifted Selka Dray as his
  first person since the spring, and tries the plate on the 29th. Orla wrote
  four lines to Jessamy Hart; no answer. Ysra Crest was a ward-holder on the 2660 Bench and
  had signed Hart's entry; she countersigned Orla's account of the hand, and has opened a page
  headed *Witnessed* with one entry. Selka Dray is unpersuaded and says so.
- **Words.** Where either could be meant the page now says "the Halden Works" or "the Municipal
  Works." Thirza Bly's line is in "the record hand." "A hold," "the hem" and "the corner" for the spawn square; "the fold" and
  "passengers" for the ward grip; "the Call"; "the Row"; "the Alderman"; "a step glass."
- **Names and pronouns introduced (author's choice, pending owner review).** Thirza Bly
  (she); Wilf Marten (he); Hesketh (she), Yarrow (he) and Foreman Birch (he) of the Halden
  license four, with Pettit (he) now on the page; Wace (he) of Old Four; Tibb and Stiles (he)
  of Graywater's ward line. The watch officers go by their watches (the Ferren is she).

## Movement Six carried state (chapters 41–48) — end of Book One

Recorded 2026-10-12 from the first draft, by its author, directly after the run. **First
draft, unreviewed:** no editorial review, cold read, measurement, repair or recheck exists.
The manuscript governs; full detail is in `provenance/MOVEMENT-006-CONTINUITY.md`. Only what
the prose shows is entered here.

- **Calendar.** Movement Six runs from the evening of Monday the 26th of the ninth month to
  Sunday the 16th of the tenth, 2680. The tenth month begins on a Saturday. The emergency was
  the night of Wednesday the 5th to Thursday the 6th (bell 11:02 p.m.; closed 12:50 a.m.).
- **The Step.** Three bells on the 26th had no tear under them; the grid's mark was moving,
  and masts were then watched by people. The Step was not walking the copper. The drums fell
  in in the order that the **Ferren main** (the storm drain) reaches them, two days apart: 19,
  20, 21, then 25, 28 and 29; drums 22–24 on the Kiln loop never did. The three stepped pumps
  make the main rise and fall four counts in and four out. Two drums quenched as a firebreak
  did not stop it, and went in inside sixty counts when filled. Nobody on the page says that
  water can step a cell, or why the bank fell in on the 17th of the ninth month.
- **The post.** The leaning post in the Old Yard is the handle of a sluice gate (a penstock in
  a brick shaft eight feet down, on a raked screw inside the post; a bar; nine turns "shuts
  the hill from the low streets"). The lane was laid over the drain, which turns there. The
  School straightened the other posts in 2390; the *Standard Lane Manual* kept the reason
  (*because the road isn't straight. S.V.*) until 2603 and lost the method; the Home Line's
  lane sheet kept the method (*Call the turn before the eighth, aloud, and say who has the
  bar. Two to the bar*). The street below the School is called Stopgate. The tenth edition
  restores all seven columns, with two lines of Kiva's in the fifth and sixth.
- **The string of the 5th.** A tear lying in the crown of Stopgate over the breathing main; a
  string "laid on its side," knots abreast eight paces apart, every knot reading, a new knot
  rising at the uphill end as the tear ran on (twelve rose; two were cut early; ten stood). It
  ignored a lit gate and turned to lit cell boxes. Where the drain dives at the School's gate
  it stopped and put an arm twenty-three paces along the wall into the open sluice shaft, and
  with the arm in the water it pumped the main. It has no name and no yield class on the page.
- **How it was answered.** Kiva took the arm off the water with the three-door sequence,
  carried it north across the lane to ground that she had tested (east was the drain), planted
  it, and gave the corner at about nine to three student still-doors (Wace, Hendry Moss,
  Yarrow); Perrin Cade and Lowen Brask shut the sluice with the bar; the Municipal Works
  stopped the pumps and quenched the Ferren bank; on Kiva's word ten blades, under the foreman's
  slate, cut the ten knots (Rhea Sorn, Kellan Renn, Jude Talley and Officer Yeo are named;
  the Halden four cut two); the Home Line closed the tear. Mara Rowan and
  twenty-four hands filled the bank with fresh cans, each salted with a pinch of plain ash
  from the Wren House reference shelf and tuned to its own count, and restarted three pumps on
  three counts. **The Ferren bank is out of step.** The pumps stood twenty-six minutes; eight
  cellars in the Hythe took a hand of water. Nobody died. Sixty-two houses and the School were
  cleared; the School's core was put down by its keeper and raised the next noon.
- **What remains.** Seven drums (19, 20, 21, 25–28) are still in step, each alone on an open
  link, to be done one a day. Drum twenty-nine was fouled by about thirty drams through a
  split plank; four streets are on boxes about a month. Saltmere's harbour bank (thirty-six
  cells) went into step in the same minute on the 17th of the ninth month and has not come
  out.
- **Body.** Kiva's left arm lost feeling to a hand above the elbow and returned over four days
  to where it was. **The first two left fingers are exactly as before; nothing restored them.**
  Aske: nothing lit until the new year. Mara's left, reading hand was starved to the wrist and
  eased inside the hour; a week before she reads a drum, a month before a bank. Perrin: two
  ribs sprung, not broken.
- **Three doors.** Three clean in all (a yard, the Pit, the Old Yard). The third was offered
  by Kiva and not asked, under a Home Line direction and Rhea Sorn's hand, with the price said
  first by her healer ("I think it goes to the elbow… I don't know"). Still uncertified,
  unclassified, and "not to be asked of her."
- **Place.** The Chair, the 14th of the tenth month: *Rowan, K., Third Division, the Crooked
  Line: seated. No longer provisional. Catcher.* Sponsor's line and all certificate conditions
  stand; "not thereby classified." The Home Line watch, the 13th: *Rowan, K. Entered on the
  Line's roll as catcher, autumn 2680. Hand as before.* (not a seat; a line-hand's day rate
  when called).
- **The offer.** Rhea Sorn's card: catcher's second to Pike on the night van, two nights a
  month from the new year to the last of the third month, dark hands, no door not on her
  certificate, Pru Finch with her for the cord, Team Seven first, "either of us may say no. No
  seat is promised." Kiva added that the *before* column be read out and that her four have
  the first word; the four signed the back on the 15th.
- **The disk.** Cold throughout. The School's "plate" is a plaster cast, labelled as taken by
  leave of the bearer; the Reader matched it to Kiva's (a wider fifth ring; a flaw across the
  third) and recognized hers as the plate of nine rings carried by the first teachers,
  released by appointment. The School's tenth edition will say so from its fourth. What it is
  for is unknown; the first book is lost.
- **The dream.** No recurrence. A second, independent account: the Ferren pump house keeper's
  book for 2604 records a three-night fever and a dream that "the roof was off the house and
  there was nothing over it," with all twenty-four needles "looking," on a night when the
  levee lamps went out with no fault. It gives no cause, meaning or use.
- **Instrument evidence.** On the 5th the notch on Kiva's pen came eleven seconds after her
  second cliff, and the lamps (every box-lit window on the hill and in the School, and
  twenty-four quenched needles at the pump house) jumped about five minutes later, when the
  hem came down. They are two things. Noa Bexley's three columns (people; rifts and spawn;
  Saltmere) all rise, and faster since the fifth month; the Chair has sent them to the Charter
  Board with Mr. Adlam second. One unvouched line says that a port overseas saw the same.
- **The season.** Read. The Regional result stands, without brackets: 1. Halden License. 2.
  Old Four. 3. Team Twenty-Eight. 4. T7. The first company goes to **the National Measure, at
  Saltmere, in the third month, 2681;** Selka Dray's mast rule goes with it under seal.
- **Team Seven.** Second step in its own right. Book: **held 25.53; sealed medical (W) 0.8,**
  opened once, for Mara Rowan's hand (1.2 drawn, entered as medical). In this movement: 3.0 on
  a second purse sheet for Pru Finch; 0.70 of call drams; 0.59 on the three-door; 4.0 lent to
  the casualty reserve and repaid; 9.0 credited from Stopgate's pot (1.8 by name to Jude
  Talley, who now has 7.8); 1.0 returned at par to Stopgate's wardens. From Stopgate: 192
  drams clean, shared civic 96, reserve 24, schools 36, teams 36; 560.4 red-tagged.
- **People.** Kellan has taken the Halden yard and stays on Seven's book. Anwen keeps her
  summer place on Mace's line and has told her mother. Perrin held 380 kilos for thirty counts
  and has his Hall's second team from the new year. Pru has her own sheet and her own red tag.
  Selka Dray called the count from a chair, on terms, and is unpersuaded. Lowen Brask was at
  the bar. Jessamy Hart has answered Orla from the south coast and asks to read what Kiva has
  written down; Kiva has not been told. Examiner Rusk gave Mara the trunk. Mara has written
  the method in seven columns for the coast boat.
- **Words.** "The Ferren main"; "a float"; "a firebreak"; "the Kiln loop"; "Stopgate"; "the
  sluice," "the penstock," "the bar," "two to the bar"; "call the turn"; "a string laid on its
  side," "abreast," "paid out"; "an arm"; "a plank" for a corner leaned between still-doors;
  "starved" (a hand that ward-trace can ease) against "turned out" (one that it cannot); "the
  *before* column"; "seated"; "the Charter Board"; "the National Measure."
- **Names and pronouns introduced (author's choice, pending owner review).** Ansty (she;
  keeper of the School's core house); Lumb (he; keeper of the Ferren pump house); Ned (he; a
  fitter's boy); Officer Yeo of Saltmere (she); Mr. Garland and his bird Admiral; Bettony
  (she; a junior). Kellan's mother signs "A. Renn, grader." The School's bursar and the junior
  with the plait are unnamed.

## PROJECT EVIDENCE — BOOK_MAP.md

# Kindling 2680 — Book One map

Status: drafting map. Opening locks resolved 2026-10-08; prose run authorized.

## 1. Book promise

Kiva Rowan opened all three known doors at thirteen and spent four years classified
below what any one of them might have earned alone. In 2680 she enters Meridian's
collegiate competition system, the regional feeder for professional rift companies.
Across one season, she must turn breadth into reliable control, learn through school
combat, train through several connected chapters to kill Riftspawn and capture their
ash, keep friendships strained by ranking, yield shares, and recruitment, and answer
a real rift event where category matters less than whether the old Home Line method
still works. Beneath that season, Kiva's recurring Unroofed Sky fever dream shows
Glory users converging as the planetary filter disappears.

## 2. Ending promise

Kiva completes the collegiate season, earns a public place without receiving a
finished explanation, and deliberately combines the doors once under rules the
reader understands. The win costs something that cannot be reset by rank. The disk
is recognized as belonging to the old bearer line; a professional company makes a
bounded recruitment offer; and evidence from the emergency shows the filter trend is
accelerating. A second historical account confirms that part of Kiva's fever dream
predates her, without explaining it. Book One closes regionally. Book Two's national
competition, energy-industry conflict, and shared-prophecy problem are opened, not
substituted for the ending.

## 3. Numerical and structural shape

- One planned historical prologue precedes Chapter 1. It must establish the EMP,
  fallout, failed restoration, first Kindlings, ash's gradual industrial adoption,
  radiation-safe nuclear recovery, ash-nuclear synthesis, distributed reactors,
  flight, Glory industry, devoted school orders, and rebuilding through ten human
  scenes without answering the reserved cause of the Fall. Its final snapshot shows
  Kiva's anomalous Kindling and first Unroofed Sky fever dream at thirteen in 2676.
  Chapter One jumps four years to the 2680 collegiate season.
- Six movements, eight chapters each; 48 chapters working total.
- Working length 270,000–315,000 words, adjusted for actual scene need.
- Approximately 87% protagonist viewpoint/focus and 13% purposeful secondary
  focus across four or five characters.
- O'Connor numerical prose targets apply: 14.6-word mean, 11-word median, about
  27.7% sentences of five words or fewer, about 3.3% of forty words or more,
  short paragraphs, accessible approximately seventh-grade readability, and
  front-loaded progression teaching.
- Major fights receive developed tactical space. No required fight count replaces
  story need, but every movement has a physical test, field exercise, or deliberate
  quiet reason.

## 4. Six movements

| Movement | Entry pressure | Learning / relationship engine | Action and turn | Exit state |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **1 — Three Doors** (Ch. 1–8) | Four years after her anomalous Kindling and fever dream, Kiva enters collegiate placement with partial training but no safe combined classification | Establish teams, standings, scholarships, pro recruitment, Mara's ash-nuclear repair work, the three traditions, and rank versus control | A placement trial combining combat, ashcraft, and rescue fails in an original way; Kiva protects someone but worsens her mark; the dream recurs as a brief physical interruption rather than guidance | Provisional lowest-division roster place; Orla takes accountable sponsorship; a pro scout notices the disk only after Kiva earns attention |
| **2 — The Crooked Lane** (Ch. 9–16) | Kiva trains inside a school system built for one-door specialists and is treated as both weak and dangerous | Several connected chapters teach spawn anatomy, kill roles, declared aborts, containment, dissolution capture, ash grading, and the team's first earned yield; rivalry, humor, and team identity develop under real fear | A supervised Handful kill and recovery lead into the first sanctioned collegiate meet: human opponents exploit habits the spawn drill created. A useful loss proves control matters more than access | Kiva earns continued roster standing; Team Seven owns its first small ash share; Noa records output, recovery efficiency, and door behavior that do not fit the institutional model |
| **3 — First Season** (Ch. 17–24) | The circuit accelerates; friends and rival advance toward recruitment, live-rift licenses, and better-funded teams on paths Kiva cannot copy | Two-door transitions and blended ashcraft are attempted and fail; teams invest earned ash in training and gear; professional footage reveals the field ceiling | A developed learning fight against an adaptive rival is followed by a live Flask response where a new spawn expression threatens the arena grid and cannot be solved like a duel | First deliberate two-door sequence works briefly; the disk warms after; the team recovers a meaningful yield; another Unroofed Sky fragment appears but supplies no tactic |
| **4 — What the Record Lost** (Ch. 25–32) | Public results and unusual spawn yields make the anomaly searchable; founder histories, ash patents, and old fever accounts disagree | Noa, Orla, and Hollis pursue different evidence. Supporting cast makes independent placement and recruitment choices while the students qualify for deeper live work | A supervised professional shadow shift becomes a real multi-type Riftspawn call. Competition habits fail until declared roles and reasons return; clean recovery after the kill matters to the threatened district | Kiva sees professional rift fighting without spectacle's safety net, learns the disk was released to a bearer, and finds a pre-Kiva Unroofed Sky fragment |
| **5 — The Regional Measure** (Ch. 33–40) | Colleges, utilities, industry houses, school orders, and professional companies want ownership, exclusion, proof, and the season's unusually rich ash yield | Friendship fractures/reforms around risk, credit, sponsorship, kill responsibility, and ash shares; Kiva trains one controlled three-door transition with abort conditions | A long bracket alternates human combat, team spawn response, rescue, and ashcraft; filter instability introduces a stronger variety no event designer ordered, forcing rival teams to cooperate | The three-door configuration is publicly real but not mastered. Kiva permanently loses fine sensation in two fingers; no ranking or recovered ash erases it |
| **6 — Why It Is Crooked** (Ch. 41–48) | A consequential rift emergency hits the collegiate district and ash grid; students and professionals must cooperate | Earlier kill drills, recovery work, ash shutdown practice, and each supporting character's independent skill become the response plan | A higher-yield regional spawn expression emerges from the failing induction/rift boundary. Kiva sequences all three doors to stabilize the boundary while professionals and peers own the developed kill, rescue, clean-ash capture, and grid tasks | Emergency answered; regional season and ash ledger close; disk recognition, a bounded pro offer, and measured proof that human potential and spawn strength are rising together open the national Book Two |

## 5. Progression design

### First third

Teach the three doors, rank/skill distinction, soul/ash limits, ash induction,
magic/ash blending, collegiate rules, professional recruitment, classification, and
institutional stakes aggressively. Make the earned-ash loop concrete: kill, contain,
recover, grade, divide, invest. Let failed attempts and demonstrations carry the
exposition. Kiva is not secretly powerful; breadth creates control problems, fatigue,
poor efficiency, and team risk before it becomes an advantage. The first supervised
spawn kills should occupy developed scenes across several chapters, including practice,
fear, tactics, dissolution, and recovery—not a montage or a single summary paragraph.

### Middle third

Reduce vocabulary repetition and let relationships, competition, and tactics carry
the system. Two-door use appears only after single-door discipline is visible.

### Final third

Surface rules at true turning points. The first intentional three-door sequence is
short, costly, and planted. It proves configuration, not mastery or Glory.

## 6. Plant/payoff ledger

| Plant | Payoff |
|---|---|
| Three doors open; classification cannot settle | Public controlled sequence in Movement 6 |
| Crooked post retained without a clear reason | Founder record explains why; protagonist applies the principle rather than worshiping the object |
| Disk warms only after Kiva's first correct deliberate sequence | Orla and Noa recognize the appointed bearer line, not a cosmic origin |
| Rival's strong single-door and ashcraft model | Rival adapts and owns an essential climax task Kiva cannot replace |
| Friend's envy or outside goal | Friendship survives only after protagonist stops treating support as automatic |
| Home Line field guide doubts school metrics | Real emergency proves both metrics and judgment are needed |
| Conflicting copies of the reasons ledger | Missing reason exposes institutional drift and guides a repair |
| Filter measurement anomaly | Final emergency produces credible evidence of acceleration, not a full realm break |
| Ash induction powers Mara's shop, the colleges, and the arena | Grid failure in Movement 6 has understood mechanisms, shutdowns, and civic stakes |
| Professional standings count rescue, closure, infrastructure, and clean ash | Kiva's first pro offer recognizes judgment rather than kill count |
| Team Seven's first small Handful yield share | The team chooses what to spend, save, and return; later rich yields strain loyalty and recruitment |
| Students and spawn both appear stronger than recent records predict | The filter admits increasing Glory pressure, raising human potential and spawn variety together |
| Kiva sees converging Glory figures and an absent sky in the 2676 fever dream | Historical fragment in Movement 4 confirms shared imagery; Books Two and Three widen the convergence |
| Prologue shows radiation rotations, nuclear-ash synthesis, compact cores, and flight | Every later grid, arena, rescue, and industrial conflict rests on understood historical mechanisms |

## 7. Movement One packet rule

The names and six opening locks in `STATE_LEDGER.md` are resolved. Movement One now
receives entry state, pressure, boundaries, output paths, and the whole eight-chapter
run—never forty-eight miniature chapter contracts.
